**India**, officially the **Republic of India**,[^cite_note-29][^cite_note-30] is a country in [South Asia](21566765.md). It is the world's [seventh-largest country by area](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_countries_and_dependencies_by_area) and its [most populous](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_countries_and_dependencies_by_population).[^cite_note-poston-sullivan-2026-31] Bounded by the [Indian Ocean](14580.md) on the south, the [Arabian Sea](2747.md) on the southwest, and the [Bay of Bengal](47892.md) on the southeast, it shares land borders with [Pakistan](23235.md) to the west,[^cite_note-33] [China](5405.md), [Nepal](171166.md), and [Bhutan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bhutan) to the north, and [Bangladesh](3454.md) and [Myanmar](19457.md) to the east. In the Indian Ocean, India is near [Sri Lanka](26750.md) and the [Maldives](19117.md).[^cite_note-34]&#x20;

[Modern humans](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Modern_humans) arrived on the [Indian subcontinent](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Indian_subcontinent) from [Africa](5334607.md) by 55,000 years ago.[^cite_note-mod-human-migration-35] [Settled life](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Neolithic) emerged in the western margins of the [Indus](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Indus_River) [river basin](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/River_basin) 9,000 years ago, evolving into the [Indus Valley Civilisation](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Indus_Valley_Civilisation) of the third millennium BCE.[^cite_note-Combined-2-36] By 1200 BCE, an [archaic form](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Proto-language) of [Sanskrit](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sanskrit), an [Indo-European language](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Indo-European_languages), [diffused](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Trans-cultural_diffusion) into India from the northwest,[^cite_note-archaic-sansk-37] its [earliest hymns](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rig_Veda) containing [elements](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Historical_Vedic_religion) of what would become [Hinduism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hinduism).[^cite_note-hinduism-elements-38] The pre-existing [Dravidian](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dravidian_languages) and other languages were gradually superseded in India's [northern plains](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Indo-Gangetic_Plain#Ganges_Plain).[^cite_note-Combined-IA-Dravidian-39] Across the central stretch of these plains, [rapid urbanisation](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Second_Urbanisation) took place, and after 500 BCE a [caste](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Caste)-based, [hereditary](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Heredity) form of [social stratification](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Social_stratification) began to solidify as [Hinduism took fuller shape](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/History_of_Hinduism#Roots_of_Hinduism).[^cite_note-late-vedic-caste-40] During this period, [Buddhism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/History_of_Buddhism_in_India) and [Jainism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/History_of_Jainism) arose, proclaiming [social orders](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Social_order) unlinked to heredity.[^cite_note-Fisher2018-67–68-41] The subsequent emergence of the loose-knit [Maurya](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Maurya_Empire) and [Gupta Empires](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gupta_Empire)[^cite_note-maurya-gupta-42] gave rise to wide-ranging cultural and scientific creativity.[^cite_note-creative-explosion-43] However, these eras saw a steady decline in women's status,[^cite_note-declining-status-44] alongside the eventual institutionalisation of [untouchability](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Untouchability).[^cite_note-gupta-untouchability-45] In [South India](678583.md), across the first millennium CE, the [Middle kingdoms](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Middle_kingdoms_of_India#The_Deccan_plateau_and_South) transmitted Dravidian language scripts and cultural traditions to [Southeast Asia](28741.md).[^cite_note-Influence_Southeast_Asia-46] Well before the millennium's end, [Judaism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/History_of_the_Jews_in_India), [Christianity](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Christianity_in_India), [Islam](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Islam_in_India), and [Zoroastrianism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Zoroastrianism_in_India) had become established on India's southern and western coasts.[^cite_note-first-millennium_coastal_religions-47]&#x20;

Early in the [2nd millennium](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2nd_millennium), Muslim armies from [Central Asia](6742.md) intermittently overran India's northern plains,[^cite_note-swift-horse-48] eventually establishing the [Delhi Sultanate](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Delhi_Sultanate) and drawing India into the [cosmopolitan networks of medieval Islam](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Islamic_Golden_Age).[^cite_note-Islamic-cosmopolis-49] The [Vijayanagara Empire](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Vijayanagara_Empire) built a powerful [military-fiscal state](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fiscal-military_state) that fostered a flourishing, distinctively South Indian Hindu culture.[^cite_note-vijayanagara-character-legacy-50] During this era, [Sikhism](27964.md) emerged in the [Punjab](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Punjab), promoting an [egalitarian](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Egalitarianism) devotion that transcended traditional Hindu and Muslim boundaries.[^cite_note-Sikhism-emergence-51] The [Mughal Empire](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mughal_Empire) ushered in two centuries of economic expansion and relative peace,[^cite_note-AsherAsher2022-187-52] leaving a lasting legacy of [administrative centralisation](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mughal_Empire#Administration_and_state)[^cite_note-metcalf-mughal-centralised-53] and [cultural synthesis](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mughal_architecture).[^cite_note-mughal-cultural-synthesis-54] The [expanding rule](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Company_rule_in_India) of the British [East India Company](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/East_India_Company) consolidated territorial control over the subcontinent[^cite_note-m\&m2012-sovereignty-55] while transforming India into a colonial economy.[^cite_note-Combined-11-56] Crown rule, or the [British Raj](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/British_Raj), began in 1858.[^cite_note-truschke-british-raj-57] Although equality before the law promised to Indians was slow to materialise,[^cite_note-Combined-12-58][^cite_note-peers-2013-59] both [British India](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Presidencies_and_provinces_of_British_India) and, to a lesser extent, the [princely states](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Princely_state)[^cite_note-roy-princely-60] saw [technological changes](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Industrial_Revolution)[^cite_note-m\&m-tech-changes-61] as [modern education](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Wood%27s_despatch) and more organised public life took root.[^cite_note-EmbreeHay1988-62][^cite_note-metcalf-publife-63] [A nationalist movement](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Indian_independence_movement) emerged in India, the first in the non-European [British Empire](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/British_Empire).[^cite_note-Marshall2001-64][^cite_note-Chiriyankandath2016-65] Noted for nonviolent resistance after 1920,[^cite_note-metcalf-metcalf-gandhi-1920-66] it was a major factor in ending British rule.[^cite_note-stein-arnold-successful-nationalism-67] In 1947, the British Indian Empire was [partitioned](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Partition_of_India) into the independent [dominions](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dominion#India_and_Pakistan) of a Hindu-majority [India](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dominion_of_India) and a Muslim-majority [Pakistan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dominion_of_Pakistan). The partition brought large-scale loss of life and unprecedented migration.[^cite_note-68]&#x20;

The [Constitution of India](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Constitution_of_India) was adopted in 1950 and declared India to be a [federal republic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Federal_republic) governed through a democratic [parliamentary system](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Parliamentary_system); it guarantees [freedom of speech](21401843.md), [freedom of press](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Freedom_of_press),[^cite_note-metcalf-freedoms-69] and [religious pluralism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Religious_pluralism).[^cite_note-Metcalf-Plural-70] Amendments enacted from [1951](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/First_Amendment_of_the_Constitution_of_India) onwards strengthened affirmative action programmes to reduce [caste inequality](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Caste_system_of_India).[^cite_note-metcalf-affirmat-71] The [reorganization of states in 1956](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/States_Reorganisation_Act,_1956) along linguistic lines institutionalised [regional language](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Regional_languages_in_India) pride[^cite_note-Metcalf-States-72] and led to the growth of [regional literature](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Indian_literature#In_modern_Indian_languages) and [cinema](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cinema_of_India).[^cite_note-raghavendra-cinema-73] During the 1970s, India reversed agricultural stagnation through the [Green Revolution](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Green_Revolution_in_India),[^cite_note-bishnu-green-74] achieving national [food security](216361.md).[^cite_note-tomlin-food-sec-75] The country also altered the regional power balance through [support for Bangladesh](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bangladesh_Liberation_War#Indian_involvement)[^cite_note-talbot-bangla-76] and enacted [constitutional reform](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Twenty-sixth_Amendment_of_the_Constitution_of_India) to end [princely privileges](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Privy_purse_in_India).[^cite_note-m\&m-privy-77] India's population grew from 361 million in 1951[^cite_note-Dyson2018-172-78] to approximately 1.464 billion in 2025.[^cite_note-unfpa-in-2025-79][^cite_note-80] Economic reforms of the 1990s integrated India's economy with the world's.[^cite_note-Tomlinson-Reforms1-81] By the 2010s, the [Indian economy](1472206.md) had a stably [high rate of growth](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_countries_by_real_GDP_growth_rate).[^cite_note-Tomlinson-Reforms2-82] A large middle class and a communications boom gained momentum.[^cite_note-talbot-middle-83] However, challenges remain, including widening [income gaps](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Economic_inequality),[^cite_note-tomlinson-income-gap-84] [gender inequality](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gender_inequality_in_India)[^cite_note-fisher-gender-85] and [environmental degradation](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Environmental_issues_in_India).[^cite_note-fisher-environ-86] In recent years, India has seen a political shift toward [majoritarianism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Majoritarianism).[^cite_note-majoritarianism-87] A [nuclear-weapon state](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/India_and_weapons_of_mass_destruction) with high [military expenditure](7171338.md), India is among the world's [largest economies](1472206.md) and a [founding member](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Foreign_relations_of_India) of the [United Nations](31769.md),[^cite_note-unfounding-mem-88] while [its cultural legacy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Culture_of_India) encompasses 45 [UNESCO World Heritage Sites](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_World_Heritage_Sites_in_India).[^cite_note-whs-sarnath-45-89] India's [megadiverse](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Megadiverse_country) land features four [biodiversity hotspots](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Biodiversity_hotspots).[^cite_note-mongobay-india-diversity-90] [Its wildlife](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Wildlife_of_India) is supported in [protected habitats](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Protected_areas_of_India) and traditionally viewed with cultural tolerance.[^cite_note-karanth-tolerance-91]&#x20;

## Etymology

Main article: [Names for India](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Names_for_India)

According to the *[Oxford English Dictionary](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Oxford_English_Dictionary)*, the English proper noun "India" derives most immediately from the [Classical Latin](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Classical_Latin) *India*, a reference to a loosely-defined historical region of Asia stretching from [South Asia](21566765.md) to the borders of China. Further [etymons](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Etymon) are: [Hellenistic Greek](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hellenistic_Greek) *India* (Ἰνδία); [Ancient Greek](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ancient_Greek) *Indos* (Ἰνδός), or the River [Indus](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Indus); Achaemenian [Old Persian](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Old_Persian) *[Hinduš](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hindu%C5%A1)* (an eastern province of the [Achaemenid Empire](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Achaemenid_Empire)); and [Sanskrit](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sanskrit) *Sindhu*, or "river," but specifically the Indus river, and by extension its well-settled basin.[^cite_note-92] The [Ancient Greeks](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ancient_Greeks) referred to South Asians as *Indoi*, 'the people of the Indus'.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEKuiper201086-93]&#x20;

The term *[Bharat](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Names_for_India#Bharat)* (*Bhārat*; pronounced [\[ˈbʱaːɾət\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Help\:IPA/Hindi_and_Urdu) [//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/transcoded/7/79/Hi-Bharat.ogg/Hi-Bharat.ogg.mp3](https://upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/transcoded/7/79/Hi-Bharat.ogg/Hi-Bharat.ogg.mp3)<sup>ⓘ</sup>), mentioned in both [Indian epic poetry](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Indian_epic_poetry) and the [Constitution of India](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Constitution_of_India),[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEClémentin-Ojha2014-94][^cite_note-95] is used in its variations by [many Indian languages](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Names_for_India). A modern rendering of the historical name *Bharatavarsha*, which applied originally to [North India](1552939.md),[^cite_note-Jha2014-96][^cite_note-FOOTNOTESingh2017\[httpsbooksgooglecombooksiddYM4DwAAQBAJpgPA253_253\]-97] *Bharat* gained increased currency from the mid-19th century as a native name for India.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEClémentin-Ojha2014-94][^cite_note-FOOTNOTEBarrow2003-98]&#x20;

*[Hindustan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hindustan)* ([\[ɦɪndʊˈstaːn\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Help\:IPA/Hindi_and_Urdu) [//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/transcoded/4/46/Hindustan.ogg/Hindustan.ogg.mp3](https://upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/transcoded/4/46/Hindustan.ogg/Hindustan.ogg.mp3)<sup>ⓘ</sup>) is a [Middle Persian](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Middle_Persian) name for India that became popular by the 13th century,[^cite_note-99] and was used widely since the era of the [Mughal Empire](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mughal_Empire). The meaning of *Hindustan* has varied, referring to a region encompassing the northern [Indian subcontinent](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Indian_subcontinent) (present-day northern India and [Pakistan](23235.md)) or to India in its near entirety.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEClémentin-Ojha2014-94][^cite_note-FOOTNOTEBarrow2003-98][^cite_note-100]&#x20;

## History

Main article: [History of India](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/History_of_India)

### Ancient India



Manuscript illustration, c. 1650, of the Sanskrit epic [Ramayana](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ramayana), composed in story-telling fashion c. 400 BCE – c. 300 CE

[^cite_note-101]



Cave 26, a [Buddhist](3267529.md) shrine, of the rock-cut [Ajanta Caves](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ajanta_Caves)

Based on [coalescence](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Coalescent_theory) of [Mitochondrial DNA](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mitochondrial_DNA) and [Y Chromosome](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Y_Chromosome) data, the earliest extant [lineages](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lineage_\(evolution\)) of [modern humans](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Homo_sapiens) on the Indian subcontinent had reached there from Africa between 80,000 and 50,000 years ago, and with high likelihood by 55,000 years ago.[^cite_note-mod-human-migration-35][^cite_note-104] [Neolithic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Neolithic) and [chalcolithic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Chalcolithic) cultures arose in the western margins of the [Indus](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Indus_River) [river basin](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/River_basin) around [Mehrgarh](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mehrgarh) in [Balochistan, Pakistan](4687.md) after 7000 BCE.[^cite_note-105] These gradually evolved into the [Indus Valley Civilisation](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Indus_Valley_Civilisation), which flourished during 2500–1900 BCE in Pakistan and western India.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEWright201045–51-106][^cite_note-Combined-2-36] Centred around cities such as [Mohenjo-daro](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mohenjo-daro), [Harappa](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Harappa) and [Dholavira](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dholavira),[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEWright2010107-107] its characteristic features included [steatite](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Soapstone) [seals](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Indus_Valley_Civilisation#Seals), a [written script](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Indus_script), [urban planning](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Harappan_architecture), terracotta human figures and animal statuettes.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEWright2010107-107][^cite_note-109] Between 1500 BCE and 1200 BCE, an [archaic form](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Proto-language) of [Sanskrit](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sanskrit), an [Indo-European language](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Indo-European_language), [diffused](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Trans-cultural_diffusion) into India from the northwest. Its evidence today is found in the [Rig Veda](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rig_Veda)—the [scripture](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Scripture) associated with the [historical Vedic religion](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Historical_Vedic_religion), one of the progenitors of what later became [Hinduism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hinduism).[^cite_note-110][^cite_note-archaic-sansk-37][^cite_note-hinduism-elements-38] The settling of the [Ganges](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ganges_river) [river plain](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/River_plain) took place during the next millennium, when swathes of the river system's adjoining regions were deforested[^cite_note-111] and prepared for agriculture.[^cite_note-112][^cite_note-FOOTNOTEDyson201816,_25-113] The [Dravidian](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dravidian_languages) and [other](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Munda_languages) languages of India were supplanted in the north, creating a broad [language family](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Language_family)-divide, with the [Indo-Aryan languages](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Indo-Aryan_languages) being spoken mainly in the [north](1552939.md) and [west](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/West_India), and the Dravidian in some parts of [east India](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/East_India) and most of the [south](678583.md).[^cite_note-Combined-IA-Dravidian-39][^cite_note-117]&#x20;

A second [urbanisation](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Urbanisation) took place in India by the middle-[first-millennium BCE](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/First_millennium_B.C.). In fortified cities in the central [Ganges plain](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Indo-Gangetic_Plain#Ganges_Plain), [social differentiation](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Social_differentiation) by [caste](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Caste), or [varna](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Varna_\(Hinduism\)), emerged.[^cite_note-late-vedic-caste-40] Also, by the mid-millennium, two new religions arose: [Jainism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/History_of_Jainism), based on the teachings of [Mahavira](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mahavira), and [Buddhism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/History_of_Buddhism_in_India), based on those of [the Buddha](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/The_Buddha). Both stressed non-violence and abjured animal sacrifices conducted in [Brahmanism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Historical_Vedic_religion#Brahmanism)[^cite_note-118] and birth into a fixed hereditary *varna*. By living ethically, lay people could rise socially and morally in these religions.[^cite_note-Fisher2018-67–68-41][^cite_note-120] The rise of the two religions was a backdrop to the emergence of the first loose-knit, geographically extensive power in South Asia, the [Maurya Empire](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Maurya_Empire).[^cite_note-maurya-gupta-42] During the rule of the founder's grandson, [Ashoka](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ashoka) (ca. 268–232 BCE), the empire briefly controlled the subcontinent's major urban hubs and arteries, except in the deep south.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEDyson201816–17-121][^cite_note-123][^cite_note-125] The empire's period was notable for creativity in art, architecture, inscriptions, and produced texts.[^cite_note-creative-explosion-43] Following his [conquest of Kalinga](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kalinga_War), in which his forces caused immense loss of life, Ashoka adopted [Buddhism](3267529.md), and subsequently commissioned numerous [rock and pillar edicts](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Edicts_of_Ashoka) throughout the Maurya Empire to promote ethical conduct, non-violence, and social welfare.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEStein201073-126][^cite_note-129]&#x20;

By the 4th and 5th centuries CE, the [Gupta Empire](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gupta_Empire)[^cite_note-maurya-gupta-42] had created a system of administration and taxation in the greater Ganges Plain;[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEKulkeRothermund2016118,_126-130] this system became a model for later Indian kingdoms.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTERobb201168–69-131] Under the Guptas, a renewed Hinduism based on devotion, rather than the management of ritual, began to assert itself.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEStein201093–94-132] The renewal was reflected in a flowering of art, literature, and science.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEGilbert201754–55-133][^cite_note-FOOTNOTEKulkeRothermund2016122–123-134] However, the eras of the two empires also witnessed a steady erosion of women's rights,[^cite_note-declining-status-44] and, by the end, the institutionalisation of [untouchability](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Untouchability).[^cite_note-gupta-untouchability-45] In South India, the [Sangam literature](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sangam_literature) of the [Tamil language](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tamil_language) reveals that, between 200 BCE and 200 CE, the southern peninsula was ruled by the [Cheras](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Chera_dynasty) and the [Cholas](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Chola_dynasty), along the western and eastern plains, respectively, of the [Kaveri river](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kaveri_river) valley, and the [Pandyas](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pandya) farther south along the [Vaigai river](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Vaigai_river) valley.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEStein201098-135] By the sixth century, the [Pallavas](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pallava) had grown into a regional power. Simultaneously, Buddhism and Jainism, which had favoured a conservative [transactionalism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Transactionalism), were replaced by kingly devotion to the gods of particular places, which became a characteristic of the [Bhakti movement](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bhakti_movement).[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEStein2010100–101-136] The Pallavas, in particular, [traded extensively with the Roman Empire](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Indo-Roman_trade_relations) and with [West](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Western_Asia) and [Southeast Asia](28741.md).[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEStein201099-137]&#x20;

### Medieval India

Main article: [Medieval India](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Medieval_India)



The [Brihadisvara Temple](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Brihadisvara_Temple), built by [Chola monarch](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Chola_Empire) [Rajaraja I](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rajaraja_I) between 1003 and 1010 CE



Calligraphy on the [Qutb Minar](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Qutb_Minar), built in the [Delhi Sultanate](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Delhi_Sultanate) from 1199 CE to 1220 CE

The Indian early medieval age, from 600 to 1200 CE, was defined by regional kingdoms and cultural diversity.[^cite_note-139][^cite_note-FOOTNOTEStein2010115–116-138] During this time, as land was cleared for agriculture, pastoral peoples and new non-traditional ruling classes were integrated into [caste](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Caste) society.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEStein2010116–117-140] The caste system consequently began to show regional differences.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEStein2010116–117-140] In the 6th and 7th centuries, the first [devotional hymns](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bhakti) were composed in Tamil.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEStein2010118-141] They were emulated across India, leading to a [resurgence of Hinduism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bhakti_movement) and laying the groundwork for the subcontinent's [regional languages](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Languages_of_India).[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEStein2010118-141] Indian royalty and the temples they supported drew large numbers of citizens to the capital cities, which grew into economic hubs.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEStein2010118–119-142][^cite_note-143] By the 8th and 9th centuries, South Indian culture and political systems spread across [Southeast Asia](28741.md).[^cite_note-144][^cite_note-Influence_Southeast_Asia-46][^cite_note-FOOTNOTEStein2010121–123-145][^cite_note-146] In the second half of the first millennium CE, [Judaism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/History_of_the_Jews_in_India), [Christianity](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Christianity_in_India), [Islam](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Islam_in_India) and [Zoroastrianism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Zoroastrianism_in_India) established roots on India's southern and western coasts.[^cite_note-first-millennium_coastal_religions-47]&#x20;

After the 10th century, [Muslim Central Asian](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Turkic_peoples#Iranian,_Indian,_Arabic,_and_Anatolian_expansion) nomadic clans—using [swift-horse](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Courser_\(horse\)) cavalry and raising vast armies united by ethnicity—repeatedly overran South Asia's north-western plains,[^cite_note-swift-horse-48] leading eventually to the establishment of the Islamic [Delhi Sultanate](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Delhi_Sultanate) in 1206,[^cite_note-delhi-sultanate-147] and drawing India into the [Islamic cosmopolis](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Islamic_Golden_Age).[^cite_note-Islamic-cosmopolis-49] The Sultanate, which came to control much of North India and made many forays into South India, initially disrupted Indian elites, but largely left its vast non-Muslim subject population to its own laws and customs.[^cite_note-zimmi-148] By repeatedly repulsing [Mongol raiders](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mongol_Empire) in the 13th century, the sultanate saved India from the devastation visited on [West](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/West_Asia) and [Central Asia](6742.md),[^cite_note-mongols-repulsed-149] setting the scene for centuries of [migration](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Human_migration) of fleeing soldiers, scholars, mystics, and artisans from those regions into the subcontinent, thereby creating a syncretic Indo-Islamic culture in the north.[^cite_note-indo-islamic-150] The sultanate's raiding and weakening of the regional kingdoms of South India paved the way for the indigenous [Vijayanagara Empire](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Vijayanagara_Empire).[^cite_note-sultanate-vijaya-151] Embracing a strong [Shaivite](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Shaivism) tradition and building upon the military technology of the sultanate, the empire came to control a large portion of peninsular India, and influenced South Indian society long after.[^cite_note-vijayanagara-character-legacy-50]&#x20;

### Early modern India



The *[dargah](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dargah)* of [Sufi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sufi) [Salim Chisti](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Salim_Chisti), built by [Mughal emperor](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mughal_Empire), [Akbar](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Akbar), early 17th century



An [East India Company rule](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Company_rule_in_India) gold [mohur](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mohur), issued during the reign of [William IIII](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/William_IV)

In the early 16th century, northern India—then mainly under Muslim rulers[^cite_note-FOOTNOTERobb201197–98-152]—fell again to the superior mobility and firepower of a new generation of Central Asian warriors.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEStein2010159-153] The resulting [Mughal Empire](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mughal_Empire) did not stamp out the local societies it came to rule. Instead, it balanced and pacified them through new administrative practices[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEAsherTalbot2022136-154][^cite_note-FOOTNOTERobb2011108–109-155] and diverse and inclusive ruling elites.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEMetcalfMetcalf201215-156] This led to more systematic, centralised, and uniform rule.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEAsherTalbot2022136,_151–152-157] By departing from tribal bonds and exclusive Islamic identity, particularly under Akbar, the Mughals united their vast empire through personal loyalty to a near-divine emperor,[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEMetcalfMetcalf201217-158] expressed through a shared Persianised culture.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEMetcalfMetcalf201217-158]&#x20;

The Mughal state derived most revenue from agriculture.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEAsherTalbot2022195-159] Its economic policies mandated tax payments in a well-regulated silver currency,[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEStein2010164-160] and integrated peasants and artisans into larger markets.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEAsherTalbot2022187-161] The relative peace the empire maintained across much of the 17th century helped drive India's economic expansion.[^cite_note-asher-talbot-akbar-162] Mughal ruling classes fostered extensive patronage of the arts, including [painting](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mughal_painting), literary forms, textiles, and [architecture](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mughal_architecture).[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEAsherTalbot2022231-163] Newly coherent social groups in northern and western India, such as the [Marathas](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Maratha_Empire), the [Rajputs](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rajput), and the [Sikhs](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sikh_Empire), gained governing ambitions during Mughal rule.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEMetcalfMetcalf201258–59-164] Through collaboration or adversity, the Mughals gave them recognition and military experience.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEMetcalfMetcalf201258–59-164] Expanding commerce gave rise to new Indian commercial and political elites along the coasts of southern and eastern India.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEMetcalfMetcalf201258–59-164] From the late 17th century, the Mughal Empire gradually began to disintegrate, and these local elites and communities frequently gained greater autonomy and self-governance.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEAsherTalbot2022330-165]&#x20;

By the early 18th century, the lines between commercial enterprise and political sovereignty had become increasingly blurred.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEAsherTalbot2022331–335-166] European chartered companies solidified their presence in India through fortified coastal outposts; most notable was the [English East India Company](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/East_India_Company).[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEAsherTalbot2022331–335-166][^cite_note-FOOTNOTEMetcalfMetcalf201278–82-167] Its control of the seas, greater resources, and more advanced military training and technology led it to assert its military strength increasingly and caused it to become attractive to a portion of the Indian elite; these factors were crucial in allowing the company to gain control over the [Bengal](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bengal) region by 1765 and sideline the other European companies.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTERobb2011115–117-168][^cite_note-FOOTNOTEAsherTalbot2022365-169][^cite_note-FOOTNOTELudden2014151–154-170][^cite_note-FOOTNOTEMetcalfMetcalf201285–90-171] Its further access to the riches of Bengal and the subsequent increased strength and size of its army enabled it to annexe or subdue most of India by the 1820s.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEMetcalfMetcalf2012103–110-172] India no longer exported manufactured goods as it long had, but instead supplied the [British Empire](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/British_Empire) with raw materials. Many historians consider this to be the onset of India's colonial period.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEAsherTalbot2022368-173] By this time, with its economic power severely curtailed by the British Parliament and having effectively been made an arm of British administration, the East India Company began more consciously to enter non-economic arenas, including education, social reform, and culture.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEAsherTalbot2022369-174]&#x20;

### Modern India

Main article: [History of India (1947–present)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/History_of_India_\(1947%E2%80%93present\))



Mahatma Gandhi after a visit to the Presidency Jail, [Calcutta](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Calcutta), April 1938



The [Capitol Complex](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Chandigarh_Capitol_Complex) in [Chandigarh](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Chandigarh), commissioned after India's [1947 partition](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Partition_of_India)

The 1848 appointment of [Lord Dalhousie](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/James_Broun-Ramsay,_1st_Marquess_of_Dalhousie) as the Governor General of the East India Company set the stage for changes essential to a modern state: consolidating and [demarcating sovereignty](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Great_Trigonometrical_Survey), monitoring the population through censuses,[^cite_note-eic-census-175] [educating citizens](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Wood%27s_despatch) and [rationalising](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rationalization_\(sociology\)) the beauraucracy.[^cite_note-tech-changes-india-europe-176] Technological advancements—among them, railways, canals, and the telegraph—were introduced within years of their introduction in Europe,[^cite_note-tech-changes-india-europe-176] with the East India Company guaranteeing [fixed financial returns](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Return_on_investment) to attract British private investors.[^cite_note-tech-changes-india-europe-176] Simultaneously, the proponents of these public works [lobbied](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lobbying) the British government for support, presenting them as necessities for military logistics and security and Indian social and cultural reform.[^cite_note-tech-changes-india-europe-176] Thus, the [Upper Doab](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Doab)'s [Ganges Canal](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ganges_Canal)—conceived by [Proby Cautley](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Proby_Cautley) under [Lord Auckland](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lord_Auckland) and completed under Dalhousie—was promoted to Company's [Court of Directors](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Board_of_Directors) in London as both a vital relief response to the [Agra famine of 1837–1838](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Agra_famine_of_1837%E2%80%931838) and a revenue-generating irrigation and navigation project. The canal, which brought agricultural prosperity to some areas,[^cite_note-ganges-canal-prosperity-177] ultimately caused soil salinisation in others.[^cite_note-m\&m-salinization-178]&#x20;

Disaffection with the company also grew during this time, culminating in the [Indian Rebellion of 1857](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Indian_Rebellion_of_1857), which swept across the central [Gangetic plain](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gangetic_Plain), and extended into Central India.[^cite_note-1857-regions-179] Shaking the foundations of Company rule,[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEMetcalfMetcalf2006100–103-180][^cite_note-FOOTNOTEBrown199485–86-181] the uprising was provoked by diverse resentments and perceptions, including invasive British-style social reforms,[^cite_note-invasive-reforms-182] harsh land taxes,[^cite_note-harsh-land-revenue-183] and summary treatment of some wealthy landowners and princes.[^cite_note-indian-elites-discontent1857-184] Yet the presumed causes of the uprising did not translate uniformly into actual rebellion.[^cite_note-rebellion-counter-examples-185] The rebel leaders proclaimed no coherent articles of faith that presaged a new political system.[^cite_note-no-new-1857-rebel-principles-186] Crucially, many regions of India—including the [Punjab](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Punjab), the [Madras](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Madras_Presidency) and [Bombay presidencies](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bombay_Presidency)—did not join the rebellion, thereby ensuring its ultimate defeat; neither did the [Bengali intelligentsia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bengal_Renaissance), nor the major [princely states](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Princely_state).[^cite_note-1857sat-out-187] After the rebellion was suppressed in 1858, the East India Company was disbanded, and the British government assumed [ direct administration of India](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/British_Raj).[^cite_note-eic-disbanded-188] Proclaiming a [unitary state](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Unitary_state) and a gradual but limited British-style parliamentary system,[^cite_note-unitary-1858-189] the new rulers also protected princes and landed gentry as a feudal safeguard against future unrest.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEMetcalfMetcalf2012103–108-190] In the decades following, a more organised public life gradually emerged across India,[^cite_note-public-life-191] eventually leading to the founding of the [Indian National Congress](149333.md) in 1885.[^cite_note-public-life-congress-192]&#x20;

Rapid commercialisation of agriculture in the second half of the 19th century brought severe economic setbacks, leaving many small farmers dependent on the uncertainties of distant markets.[^cite_note-whims-distant-markets-193] This period saw a rise in the frequency of large-scale [famines](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Famine_in_India);[^cite_note-stein-famines-194] moreover, despite the risks of infrastructure development being borne by Indian taxpayers, little industrial employment was generated for the local population.[^cite_note-risk-return-Raj-195] However, commercial cropping, especially in the newly canal-irrigated Punjab, did increase food production for internal consumption.[^cite_note-punjab-canals-196] The expanding railway network provided critical famine relief,[^cite_note-metcalf-famine-relief-197] notably reduced the cost of moving goods,[^cite_note-metcalf-famine-relief-197] and helped nascent Indian-owned industry.[^cite_note-M\&M-railways-indian-industry-198] The famines were systematically documented, leading to the publication of the [Indian Famine Codes](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Indian_Famine_Codes) and reports of the Indian Famine Commissions of 1880, 1897, and 1901.[^cite_note-dreze-famine-prevention-199]&#x20;

During [World War I](4764461.md), more than [one million Indians served](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Indian_Army_during_World_War_I) and Indian land taxes made substantial financial contributions to the [Allied](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Allies_of_World_War_I) war effort.[^cite_note-first-world-war-200] After the war, the British enacted [limited constitutional reforms](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Montagu%E2%80%93Chelmsford_Reforms) but also [repressive legislation](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rowlatt_act), galvanising [Mahatma Gandhi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mahatma_Gandhi), newly arrived from South Africa, to launch a [nonviolent](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nonviolent) [non-cooperation movement](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Non-cooperation_movement_\(1919%E2%80%931922\)).[^cite_note-metcalf-gandhi-novel-protest-201] In the late 1920s and early 1930s, ideas from [Bolshevism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bolshevism) and [anarchism](12.md) electrified a growing militancy across the subcontinent and injected a new urgency into the Congress's civil-disobedience movement.[^cite_note-raza-lead-1-202] Starting in the mid-1930s, the British introduced legislative reforms including special representation for India's [untouchables](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Untouchability), and the [Government of India Act of 1935](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Government_of_India_Act_of_1935), a blueprint for a [federated state](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Federation) with extensive provincial autonomy;[^cite_note-communalaward-goi35-203] the Indian National Congress won victories in the resulting elections.[^cite_note-37-elections-204] The next decade was punctuated by escalating crises: the [British Viceroy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Victor_Hope,_2nd_Marquess_of_Linlithgow)'s unilateral declaration of [India's entry ino World War II](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/India_in_World_War_II), the resignation of the provincial Congress ministries, the party's [1942 push for non-cooperation](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Quit_India_Movement),[^cite_note-ww2-1942-205] the subsequent jailing of its leaders, and an upsurge of [Muslim nationalism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Muslim_nationalism_in_South_Asia).[^cite_note-muslim-nationalism-206] After the war, the [Muslim League](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/All-India_Muslim_League)'s sweeping success in the [1946 provincial elections](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1946_Indian_provincial_elections) was a critical turning point; by securing the Muslim-reserved seats, it established a Muslim mandate for Pakistan that led eventually to the [partition of British India](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Partition_of_India).[^cite_note-1946-elections-Pakistan-207] Two new nations, Pakistan and India, came into existence on 14–15 August 1947, accompanied by widespread loss of life and an unprecedented migration.[^cite_note-partition-stack-208]&#x20;

The [Constitution of India](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Constitution_of_India) was adopted in 1950 and declared India to be a [federal republic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Federal_republic) governed through a democratic [parliamentary system](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Parliamentary_system); it guaranteed [freedom of speech](21401843.md), [freedom of press](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Freedom_of_press),[^cite_note-metcalf-freedoms-69] and [religious pluralism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Religious_pluralism).[^cite_note-Metcalf-Plural-70] Amendments enacted from [1951](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/First_Amendment_of_the_Constitution_of_India) onwards strengthened affirmative action programmes to dismantle [caste system](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Caste_system_of_India) inequalities.[^cite_note-metcalf-affirmat-71] The [States Reorganisation Act of 1956](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/States_Reorganisation_Act,_1956), which redrew provincial boundaries along linguistic lines, institutionalised [regional language](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Regional_languages_in_India) pride[^cite_note-Metcalf-States-72] and led to the growth of regional language literature and cinema.[^cite_note-raghavendra-cinema-73] In 1971, the creation of [Bangladesh](3454.md) altered the regional power balance;[^cite_note-talbot-bangla-76] the abolition of [princely privileges](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Privy_purse_in_India#Abolition) removed the last vestiges of India's feudal order.[^cite_note-m\&m-privy-77][^cite_note-fisher-princely-209] During this time, India's population grew rapidly: it rose from 361 million in 1951,[^cite_note-Dyson2018-172-78] to approximately 1.464 billion in 2025,[^cite_note-unfpa-in-2025-79] surpassing China's in 2021.[^cite_note-poston-sullivan-2026-31] Economic reforms of the 1990s integrated India's economy with the world's, reducing government regulations on domestic and foreign private businesses.[^cite_note-Tomlinson-Reforms1-81] By the mid-2010s, India's [economy](1472206.md) was [rapidly growing](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_countries_by_real_GDP_growth_rate),[^cite_note-Tomlinson-Reforms2-82] most notably in [information technology](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Information_technology_in_India) services.[^cite_note-tomlinson2016-it-growth-210] India had a large, consumption-driven middle class and witnessed a communications boom.[^cite_note-talbot-middle-83] However, India's economic growth has been accompanied by widening [income gaps](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Income_gap),[^cite_note-tomlinson-income-gap-84] gender inequality,[^cite_note-fisher-gender-85] and degradation of the environment.[^cite_note-fisher-environ-86] In recent years, India has witnessed a shift toward [majoritarianism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Majoritarianism) and [Hindu nationalism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hindu_nationalism),[^cite_note-majoritarianism-87] which has led to [democratic erosion](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Democratic_backsliding).[^cite_note-democratic-erosion-211]&#x20;

## Geography

### Geology

Main article: [Geology of India](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Geology_of_India)

India accounts for the bulk of the Indian subcontinent, lying atop the [Indian tectonic plate](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Indian_Plate), a part of the [Indo-Australian Plate](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Indo-Australian_Plate).[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEAliAitchison2005-212] India's defining geologic processes began approximately 70 million years ago, when the Indian Plate, then part of the southern supercontinent [Gondwana](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gondwana), began a north-eastward [drift](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Plate_tectonics) caused by [seafloor spreading](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Seafloor_spreading) to its south-west, and later, south and south-east.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEAliAitchison2005-212] Simultaneously, the vast [Tethyan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tethys_Ocean) [oceanic crust](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Oceanic_crust), to its northeast, began to [subduct](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Subduction) under the [Eurasian Plate](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Eurasian_Plate).[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEAliAitchison2005-212] The [Indian continental crust](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Indian_plate) was obstructed and was sheared horizontally; while its lower crust and mantle slid beneath Eurasia, the upper layer piled up in [sheets](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nappe) ahead of the subduction zone.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEMolnar2015117-213] This mountain-building [orogeny](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Orogeny) created the Himalayas.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEMolnar2015118-214] The middle and stiffer layer continued to push into Tibet, causing [crustal thickening](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Thrust_tectonics) of the [Tibetan Plateau](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tibetan_Plateau).[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEMolnar2015128-215] Immediately south of the emerging Himalayas, plate movement created a vast crescent-shaped [trough](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Trough_\(geology\)) that rapidly filled with river-borne sediment[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEDikshitSchwartzberg20237-216] and now constitutes the [Indo-Gangetic Plain](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Indo-Gangetic_Plain).[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEPrakash_et_al.2000-217] The original Indian plate makes its first appearance above the sediment in the ancient [Aravalli range](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Aravalli_range), which extends from the [Delhi Ridge](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Delhi_Ridge) in a southwesterly direction. To the west lies the [Thar Desert](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Thar_Desert), whose eastern spread is checked by the Aravallis.[^cite_note-aravalli-218][^cite_note-prasad-aravalli-219][^cite_note-fisher-aravalli-220]&#x20;



A panoramic view of the Garhwal and Kumaon Himalayas. Peaks rising above their surroundings are, among others, [Trisul](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Trisul), [Nanda Devi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nanda_Devi), the highest peak entirely within India's borders, and [Nanda Kot](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nanda_Kot). The [Tibetan Plateau](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tibetan_Plateau) lies behind these mountains, as does the part of the [Indus-Yarlung suture zone](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Indus-Yarlung_suture_zone), the contour along which the [Indian Plate](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Indian_Plate) has welded to the [Eurasian plate](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Eurasian_plate). [Mount Kailash](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mount_Kailash) and [Lake Manasarovar](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lake_Manasarovar) in the [Tibet Trans-Himalaya](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Transhimalaya)—sacred in Hindu and Buddhist mythology—lie immediately behind to the right. The [Indus](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Indus) and [Yarlung Tsangpo](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Yarlung_Tsangpo) (the upper [Brahmaputra](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Brahmaputra) river), which mark the western and eastern limits of the Himalaya range, rise near the lake.

### Physical Geography

Main article: [Geography of India](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Geography_of_India)

The Indian Plate survives as [peninsular India](678583.md), the oldest and geologically most stable part of the country. It extends as far north as the [Satpura](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Satpura_Range) and [Vindhya](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Vindhya_Range) ranges in central India. These parallel chains run from the Arabian Sea coast in Gujarat in the west to the coal-rich [Chota Nagpur Plateau](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Chota_Nagpur_Plateau) in Jharkhand in the east.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEDikshitSchwartzberg20238-221] To the south, the peninsular landmass, the [Deccan Plateau](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Deccan_Plateau), is flanked on the west and east by coastal ranges known as [Western](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Western_Ghats) and [Eastern Ghats](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Eastern_Ghats);[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEDikshitSchwartzberg20239–10-222] the plateau contains the country's oldest rock formations, some over one billion years old. Geographically, India lies to the north of the equator between 6° 44′ and 35° 30′ north latitude[^cite_note-223] and 68° 7′ and 97° 25′ east longitude.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEMinistry_of_Information_and_Broadcasting20071-224]&#x20;

Major Himalayan-origin rivers that substantially flow through India include the [Ganges](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ganges) and the [Brahmaputra](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Brahmaputra_River), both of which drain into the [Bay of Bengal](47892.md).[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEDikshitSchwartzberg202315-225] Important tributaries of the Ganges include the [Yamuna](354286.md) and the [Kosi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kosi_River). The Kosi's extremely low gradient, caused by long-term silt deposition, leads to severe floods and course changes.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEDuff1993353-226][^cite_note-FOOTNOTEBasuXavier2017\[httpsbooksgooglecombooksidnXmLDgAAQBAJpgPA78_78\]-227] Major peninsular rivers, whose steeper gradients prevent extensive flooding, include the [Godavari](47988.md), the [Mahanadi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mahanadi_River), the [Kaveri](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kaveri_River), and the [Krishna](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Krishna_River), which also drain into the Bay of Bengal;[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEDikshitSchwartzberg202316-228] and the [Narmada](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Narmada_River) and the [Tapti](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tapti_River), which drain into the [Arabian Sea](2747.md).[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEDikshitSchwartzberg202317-229]&#x20;

India's coastline measures 7,517 kilometres (4,700 mi) in length; of this distance, 5,423 kilometres (3,400 mi) belong to peninsular India and 2,094 kilometres (1,300 mi) to the Andaman, Nicobar, and Lakshadweep island chains.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEKumarPathakPednekarRaju2006-230] According to the Indian naval hydrographic charts, the [mainland coastline](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Coastal_India) consists of 43% sandy beaches, 11% rocky shores including cliffs, and 46% [mudflats](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mudflat) or marshy shores.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEKumarPathakPednekarRaju2006-230] Coastal features include the marshy [Rann of Kutch](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rann_of_Kutch) of western India and the alluvial [Sundarbans](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sundarbans) delta of eastern India, the latter shared with Bangladesh.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEDikshitSchwartzberg202312-231] India has two [archipelagos](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Archipelago): the [Lakshadweep](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lakshadweep) [coral atolls](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Atoll) off the south-western coast, and the Andaman and Nicobar Islands, a volcanic chain in the [Andaman Sea](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Andaman_Sea).[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEDikshitSchwartzberg202313-232]&#x20;

### Climate



[Köppen climate classification](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/K%C3%B6ppen_climate_classification) of India

In [Köppen climate classification](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/K%C3%B6ppen_climate_classification) the regions of India are: [Dry-winter, humid sub-tropical](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/K%C3%B6ppen_climate_classification#Cwa:_Dry-winter_humid_subtropical_climate) (CWa, largely the river plains of the Ganges, Brahmaputra, and Punjab rivers); [Tropical savanna with dry winters](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/K%C3%B6ppen_climate_classification#Aw:_Tropical_savanna_climate_with_dry_winters) (Aw, large parts of peninsular India except the [Deccan Plateau](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Deccan_Plateau) and the [Western Ghats](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Western_Ghats)); [Hot semi-arid](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/K%C3%B6ppen_climate_classification#BSh:_Hot_semi-arid) (BSh, Deccan plateau, parts of Gujarat, eastern Rajasthan, Punjab, and Western Uttar Pradesh); [Hot Desert](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/K%C3%B6ppen_climate_classification#BWh:_Hot_deserts) (BWh, northern Gujarat and western Rajasthan); [Tropical Monsoon](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/K%C3%B6ppen_climate_classification#Am:_Tropical_monsoon_climate) (Am, Western Ghats), [Dry winter sub-topical highland](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/K%C3%B6ppen_climate_classification#Cwb:_Dry-winter_subtropical_highland_climate) (CWb, Himachal Pradesh, Uttarakhand, northern Bengal and upper [northeast India](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Northeast_India)), [Cold desert](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/K%C3%B6ppen_climate_classification#BWk:_Cold_deserts) (BWk, Eastern Ladakh), [Tropical rainforest](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/K%C3%B6ppen_climate_classification#Af:_Tropical_rainforest_climate) (Af, [Sundarbans](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sundarbans_Biosphere_Reserve), [Andaman and Nicobar Islands](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Andaman_and_Nicobar_Islands); [Warm summer hemiboreal](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/K%C3%B6ppen_climate_classification#Dfb/Dwb/Dsb:_Warm_summer_humid_continental/hemiboreal_climates) (Dsb, upper Himachal Pradesh and Kashmir below the Himalayas), and [Ice cap](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/K%C3%B6ppen_climate_classification#EF:_Ice_cap_climate) in the [Western Himalayas](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Western_Himalayas).&#x20;

Monsoon weather systems play a significant role in India's climate. In turn, the Himalayas and the Tibetan Plateau play an important role in creating the South Asian monsoon, which accounts for 75 to 80 per cent of India's annual rain.[^cite_note-rain_percent_monsoon-234] In winter, the Tibetan Plateau (average altitude 4500 m) acts like a tower of ice and splits the [westerlies](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Westerlies), both the low-level and, by friction, the high-altitude [jet streams](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jet_stream).[^cite_note-barry-hallmck-winter-split-235] The southern branch rounds the Himalayas. Just beyond, as it slows down and creates a [convergence](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Convergence_zone), or backup, the air [sinks](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Subsidence_\(atmosphere\)), creating dry, northeasterly surface winds over India. This maintains dry, cool, Indian winters.[^cite_note-subsidence-winter-236] After these winds slow down, sub-tropical westerlies guide extratropical [Western disturbances](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Western_disturbance) from the [Mediterranean Region](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mediterranean_Region) and the [Middle East](19323.md) across northwestern India, bringing rain for winter crops and replenishing the glaciers in the [Karakorams](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Karakoram).[^cite_note-western_disturbance-237]&#x20;

By May, the sun bakes the Tibetan Plateau. Rising warm air generates a high-altitude [anticyclone](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/High-pressure_area).[^cite_note-tibet-baking-238] This creates the [Tropical Easterly Jet Stream](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tropical_Easterly_Jet) over southern India, which draws moisture-laden southwesterly surface winds off the Indian Ocean.[^cite_note-tropical-easterly-239] The Western Ghats intercept these winds, which release their moisture as [orographic rain](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Precipitation_types#Orographic), leaving a large swath of peninsular India in the mountains' [rain shadow](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rain_shadow).[^cite_note-orographic-wghats-240] India’s interior plains rely on [monsoon depressions](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Monsoon_trough#Monsoon_depressions) for their rain. These low-pressure storm systems form over the Bay of Bengal and are steered clockwise and inland by the anticyclonic jet stream.[^cite_note-monsoon-onset-bay-bengal-241] They cause summer rainfall across India from June to September.[^cite_note-june-september-monsoon-242] However, rainfall fluctuates in cycles, alternating between active phases characterised by heavy rain and dry breaks.[^cite_note-monsoon-cycles-243]&#x20;

### Biodiversity

Main articles: [Forestry in India](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Forestry_in_India) and [Wildlife of India](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Wildlife_of_India)

India is a [megadiverse country](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Megadiverse_country), a term employed for 17 countries that host high [biological diversity](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Biological_diversity) and contain many species [indigenous](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Indigenous_\(ecology\)), or [endemic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Endemic), to them.[^cite_note-244] India is the [habitat](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Habitat) for 8.6% of all [mammals](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mammal), 13.7% of [bird](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bird) species, 7.9% of [reptile](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Reptile) species, 6% of [amphibian](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Amphibian) species, 12.2% of [fish](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fish) species, and 6.0% of all [flowering plant](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Flowering_plant) species.[^cite_note-245][^cite_note-Puri-246] Fully a third of Indian plant species are endemic.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEBasak198324-247] India also overlaps four of the world's 34 [biodiversity hotspots](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Biodiversity_hotspot),[^cite_note-IUCN-India-248] or regions that display significant habitat loss in the presence of high endemism.[^cite_note-250][^cite_note-SivaperumanVenkataraman2018-249]&#x20;

India's densest forests, such as the [tropical moist forest](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tropical_and_subtropical_moist_broadleaf_forest) of the [Andaman Islands](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Andaman_Islands), the [Western Ghats](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Western_Ghats), and [Northeast India](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Northeast_India), occupy about 3% of its land area.[^cite_note-Jha2018-251][^cite_note-indiaforest-252] *Moderately dense forest*, whose canopy density is between 40% and 70%, occupies 9.39% of India's land area.[^cite_note-Jha2018-251][^cite_note-indiaforest-252] It predominates in the [temperate coniferous forest](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Temperate_coniferous_forest) of the [Himalayas](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Himalaya), the moist deciduous *[sal](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Shorea_robusta)* forest of eastern India, and the dry deciduous [teak](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Teak) forest of central and southern India.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTETritsch200111–12-253] India has two natural zones of [thorn forest](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Deserts_and_xeric_shrublands), one in the [Deccan Plateau](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Deccan_Plateau), immediately east of the Western Ghats, and the other in the western part of the Indo-Gangetic plain, now turned into rich agricultural land by irrigation, its features no longer visible.[^cite_note-254] Among the Indian subcontinent's notable indigenous trees are the [astringent](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Astringent) *[Azadirachta indica](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Azadirachta_indica)*, or *neem*, which is widely used in rural Indian [herbal medicine](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Herbal_medicine),[^cite_note-Goyal2006-255] and the luxuriant *[Ficus religiosa](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ficus_religiosa)*, or *peepul*,[^cite_note-Hughes2013-256] which is displayed on the ancient seals of [Mohenjo-daro](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mohenjo-daro),[^cite_note-AmeriCostello2018-257] and under which [the Buddha](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gautama_Buddha) is recorded in the [Pali canon](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/P%C4%81li_Canon) to have sought enlightenment.[^cite_note-Gwynne2011-258]&#x20;

Many Indian species have descended from those of [Gondwana](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gondwana), the southern [supercontinent](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Supercontinent) from which India separated more than 100 million years ago.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTECrameOwen2002142-259] India's subsequent collision with Eurasia set off a mass exchange of species. However, [volcanism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Deccan_Traps) and [climatic changes](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Climate_variability_and_change) later caused the extinction of many endemic Indian forms.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEKaranth2006-260] Still later, mammals entered India from Asia through two [zoogeographic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Zoogeography) passes flanking the Himalayas.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTETritsch200114-261] This lowered endemism among India's mammals, which stands at 12.6%, contrasting with 45.8% among reptiles and 55.8% among amphibians.[^cite_note-Puri-246] Among endemics are the vulnerable[^cite_note-262] [hooded leaf monkey](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nilgiri_Langur)[^cite_note-itis-263] and the threatened [Beddome's toad](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Duttaphrynus_beddomii)[^cite_note-IUCN-264][^cite_note-265] of the Western Ghats.&#x20;

According to the [IUCN Red List](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/IUCN_Red_List), India contains 1,021 threatened animal species—99 [mammal](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mammal), 82 [bird](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bird), 105 [reptile](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Reptile), 139 [amphibian](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Amphibian), 332 [fish](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fish), 10 [mollusk](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mollusk), and 254 other [invertebrate](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Invertebrate)—727 threatened plant species, and 4 threatened [fungi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fungi) species.[^cite_note-iucn-red-india-266] Among them are the endangered [Bengal tiger](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bengal_tiger) and the [Ganges river dolphin](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/South_Asian_river_dolphin). [Critically endangered](7451605.md) species include the [gharial](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gharial), a [crocodilian](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Crocodilian); the [great Indian bustard](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Great_Indian_bustard); and the [Indian white-rumped vulture](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/White-rumped_vulture), which has become nearly extinct by having ingested the carrion of [diclofenac](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Diclofenac)-treated cattle.[^cite_note-LovetteFitzpatrick2016-267] Before they were extensively used for agriculture and cleared for human settlement, the thorn forests of Punjab were mingled at intervals with open grasslands that were grazed by large herds of [blackbuck](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Blackbuck_Conservation_Area) preyed on by the [Asiatic cheetah](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Asiatic_cheetah); the blackbuck, no longer extant in Punjab, is now severely endangered in India, and the cheetah is extinct.[^cite_note-268] The pervasive and ecologically devastating human encroachment of recent decades has critically endangered Indian wildlife. In response, the system of [national parks](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/National_parks_of_India) and [protected areas](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Protected_areas_of_India), first established in 1935, was expanded substantially. In 1972, India enacted the [Wildlife Protection Act](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Wildlife_Protection_Act_of_1972)[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEMinistry_of_Environment_and_Forests_1972-269] and [Project Tiger](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Project_Tiger) to safeguard crucial wilderness; the Forest Conservation Act was enacted in 1980 and amendments added in 1988.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEDepartment_of_Environment_and_Forests1988-270] India hosts [more than five hundred wildlife sanctuaries](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Wildlife_sanctuaries_of_India) and [eighteen biosphere reserves](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Biosphere_reserves_of_India),[^cite_note-271] four of which are part of the [World Network of Biosphere Reserves](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/World_Network_of_Biosphere_Reserves); its [eighty-nine wetlands](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_Ramsar_sites_in_India) are registered under the [Ramsar Convention](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ramsar_Convention).[^cite_note-272]&#x20;

## Government and politics

|  | This article is **actively undergoing a [major edit](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Wikipedia\:Edit_lock)** for a little while.
