Main articles: [Politics of India](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Politics_of_India), [Government of India](553883.md), [State governments of India](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/State_governments_of_India), and [Local government in India](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Local_government_in_India)

See also: [Democracy in India](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Democracy_in_India) and [Constitution of India](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Constitution_of_India)

Citizenship

During the [Constituent Assembly of India](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Constituent_Assembly_of_India) debates between 1946 and 1949, India's founders strongly preferred *[Jus soli](54035.md)*, or citizenship based on birth on the soil, viewing it as more democratic than basing rights on *[Jus sanguinis](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jus_sanguinis)*, or descent-based citizenship, which they considered racial and outdated.[^cite_note-citizenship-const-assembly-274] Although [ India's 1950 constitution](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Constitution_of_India) originally granted exceptions to those who had migrated to India after the [Partition](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Partition_of_India), the [Citizenship Act of 1955](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Indian_nationality_law) formally established birthplace as the foundation for Indian citizenship.[^cite_note-partition-citizenship-act-275] However, in the 1980s, discontent in the [northeastern state](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Northeast_India) of [Assam](186162.md), both [political](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Assam_Movement) and [ethnic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bongal_Kheda), which was directed against [Bengali-speaking](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bengali_language) migrants, led to several legislative amendments that eroded *jus soli*. India's [new citizenship laws](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Citizenship_\(Amendment\)_Act,_2019) have shifted to favour bloodline over birthplace, creating legal obstacles for new or marginalised groups to experience [substantive equality](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Substantive_equality).[^cite_note-jayal-citizenship-276]&#x20;

The Houses of Parliament

The [Lok Sabha](275456.md), India’s lower house of parliament, has 543 members directly elected by citizens aged 18 and older, with specific seats reserved to ensure fair representation for [Scheduled Castes and Scheduled Tribes](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Scheduled_Castes_and_Scheduled_Tribes).[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEMadhavan2024-277] However, its legislative productivity has significantly declined over the decades, with the average number of working days dropping from 121 days per year in the 1950s to 68 days between 2000 and 2023.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEMadhavan2024-277] In addition, more meeting time is lost to disruptions.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEMadhavan2024-277] The [Rajya Sabha](232210.md), India’s upper house of parliament, serves as a [federal chamber](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Federation) where [state legislatures](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Vidhan_Sabha) elect members through a system that balances state size with minimum representation for smaller regions.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEShastri2024-278] However, historical data shows that most members serve only a single term, suggesting that political party leaders sometimes use these seats to reward favourites.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEShastri2024-278]&#x20;

The Cabinet

The [Cabinet](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cabinet_\(government\)), or in India, the [Union Council of Ministers](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Union_Council_of_Ministers), is the principal institution of [governance](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Governance).[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEMehra2024-279] It is traditionally defined by three ideal attributes: collective responsibility, political homogeneity, and a [prime minister](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Prime_minister) who acts as [first among equals](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Primus_inter_pares).[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEMehra2024-279] The early administrations of [Jawaharlal Nehru](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jawaharlal_Nehru), from 1946 until the mid-1950s, prominently included confident, independent-minded ministers—such as [Sardar Patel](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sardar_Patel), the Deputy Prime Minister; [Maulana Azad](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Maulana_Azad), the education minister; [Baldev Singh](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Baldev_Singh), the defence minister; and [B.R. Ambedkar](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/B.R._Ambedkar), the chairman of the drafting committee of India's Constitution and the Law Minister—who were able to express disagreeing views.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEMehra2024-279] However, after the 1957 election, the Congress's overwhelming mandate shifted governance to a centralised prime ministerial model.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEMehra2024-279] India's humiliating defeat in the [India-China War](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/India-China_War) of 1962 has been attributed to a lack of balancing political advice.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEMehra2024-279] After Nehru's death in 1964, the brief tenure of prime minister [Lal Bahadur Shastri](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lal_Bahadur_Shastri) saw a partial swing back to cabinet governance.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEMehra2024-279] By 1969, however, under [Indira Gandhi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Indira_Gandhi), the central government was operating largely in the prime minister's shadow.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEMehra2024-279] This resulted in decisions such as the imposition of [the Emergency](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/The_Emergency_\(India\)), a two-year suspension of India's civil liberties during 1975–1977.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEMehra2024-279] In contrast, the subsequent [Janata Party administration](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Premiership_of_Morarji_Desai) of [Morarji Desai](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Morarji_Desai), 1977–1979, swung toward cabinet-based governance, though it lacked ideological cohesion.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEMehra2024-279] After Indira Gandhi's death in 1984, [Rajiv Gandhi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rajiv_Gandhi)'s administration was characterised by an overwhelming legislative majority that allowed the prime minister to reshuffle portfolios frequently.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEMehra2024-279] Between 1979 and 2014, India experienced eleven coalition cabinets where the ideal of political homogeneity was severely tested by the competing demands of various partners.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEMehra2024-279] During this era, alliance members bargained for specific portfolios and dictated their own appointments, which significantly compromised the prime minister's authority.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEMehra2024-279] Three prime ministers who served a full five-year term or more were [P. V. Narasimha Rao](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/P._V._Narasimha_Rao), who served from 1991 to 1996; [Atal Bihari Vajpayee](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Atal_Bihari_Vajpayee), from 1999 to 2004; and [Manmohan Singh](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Manmohan_Singh), from 2004 until 2014. During the latter's tenure, two centres of decision-making emerged: the Prime Minister and the Chairperson of the [Indian National Congress](149333.md), [Sonia Gandhi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sonia_Gandhi). Recent [National Democratic Alliance](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/National_Democratic_Alliance) majorities in 2014 and 2019 have returned substantial control of the government to the prime minister, [Narendra Modi](444222.md).[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEMehra2024-279]&#x20;

The Political Parties

Since the mid-1980s—marking the second half of India's postcolonial history—the [Bhartiya Janata Party](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bhartiya_Janata_Party), or BJP, has grown from a marginal party into a political giant.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEJaffrelot2024-280] It has won consecutive national victories in 2014 and 2019, and controls a record number of state governments.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEJaffrelot2024-280] Scholars interpret this rapid expansion as a form of [hegemony](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hegemony) where [Hindu nationalism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hindu_nationalism) has largely sidelined traditional [secularism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Secularism) in India.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEJaffrelot2024-280] Along with its ideological draw, the party's deep pockets and heavy hand on the mainstream media and watchdogs—like the [Supreme Court](395852.md) and the [Election Commission](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Election_Commission)—have hemmed in opposition parties.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEJaffrelot2024-280] This political hold relies largely on Narendra Modi's top-down leadership and his broad popularity with Indian voters.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEJaffrelot2024-280] Unlike the BJP, the [Congress Party](149333.md) relied on a [centrist](39619035.md) platform and a loose organisation, but it has struggled to fit a changing, right-leaning electorate.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEHasan2024-281] The Congress Party shifts between compromising its beliefs and directly attacking [Hindutva](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hindutva); this has blocked its path to winning back its traditional [civic nationalism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Civic_nationalism).[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEHasan2024-281] Fearing voter backlash, Congress leaders have largely avoided a united stand for secularism and [social justice](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Social_justice).[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEHasan2024-281] They have failed to build a steady [grassroots movement](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Grassroots_movement) to stand against the BJP's platform.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEHasan2024-281] From the late 1980s until 2014, [regional parties](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Regionalism_\(politics\)) wielded clout nationwide by propping up coalition governments and holding the keys to making or breaking central rule. During this period of fractured politics, breakaway groups from national parties like Congress and the Janata Dal ruled a vast majority of Indian states. However, the rise of back-to-back single-party majorities in the Lok Sabha since 2014 has sharply reduced these local players' nationwide leverage and cabinet seats. While the ruling party still reaches out to local groups to pass laws in the upper house, these homegrown parties no longer hold the power to shake or topple the central government. Among India's major regional parties are: [Dravida Munnetra Kazhagam](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dravida_Munnetra_Kazhagam) (DMK), [Telugu Desam Party](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Telugu_Desam_Party) (TDP), [Telugu Desam Party](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Telugu_Desam_Party) (YSRCP), and [Bharat Rashtra Samithi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bharat_Rashtra_Samithi) (BRS) in [South India](678583.md); [All India Trinamool Congress](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/All_India_Trinamool_Congress) (TMC), [Biju Janata Dal](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Biju_Janata_Dal) (BJK), [Janata Dal (United)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Janata_Dal_\(United\)) (JD(U)), and [Rashtriya Janata Dal](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rashtriya_Janata_Dal) (RJD) in [East](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/East_India) and [Northeast India](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Northeast_India); and [Bahujan Samaj Party](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bahujan_Samaj_Party) (BSP), [Samajwadi Party](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Samajwadi_Party) (SP), [Shiv Sena](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Shiv_Sena), [Nationalist Congress Party](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nationalist_Congress_Party) (NCP), [Aam Aadmi Party](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Aam_Aadmi_Party) (AAP), [Shiromani Akali Dal](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Shiromani_Akali_Dal) (SAD), [Jammu & Kashmir National Conference](512306.md) (JKNC) and the [Jammu and Kashmir Peoples Democratic Party](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jammu_and_Kashmir_Peoples_Democratic_Party) (JKPDP).&#x20;

\
&#x20;

\
&#x20;India is a [parliamentary republic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Parliamentary_republic) with a [multi-party system](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Multi-party_system).[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEBurnellCalvert1999125-282] There are six recognised [national parties](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_political_parties_in_India#National_parties) in the country, including the [Indian National Congress](149333.md) (generally, "the Congress") and the [Bharatiya Janata Party](149330.md) (BJP); there are over 50 [regional parties](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_political_parties_in_India#State_parties).[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEElection_Commission_of_India-283] The Congress is considered the [ideological centre](39619035.md) in Indian [political culture](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Political_culture);[^cite_note-284] the BJP is [right-wing](56522.md) to [far-right](221220.md).&#x20;



[Narendra Modi](444222.md) has been India's Prime Minister since 2014.

Following independence on 15 August 1947, Jawaharlal Nehru served as prime minister of the [Dominion of India](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dominion_of_India), remaining caretaker until 1951, shortly after India became a republic in 1950. Led by Nehru, the Congress won the [1951](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1951_Indian_general_election), [1957](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1957_Indian_general_election), and [1962](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1962_Indian_general_election) general elections comfortably. After Nehru died in May 1964, [Lal Bahadur Shastri](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lal_Bahadur_Shastri) became prime minister. Shastri died in January 1966, shortly after signing the [Tashkent Peace Declaration](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tashkent_Declaration).[^cite_note-286] The Congress chose [Indira Gandhi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Indira_Gandhi) as prime minister, leading the party to election victories in [1967](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1967_Indian_general_election) and [1971](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1971_Indian_general_election). In 1975, she declared a [state of emergency](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/The_Emergency_\(India\)), suspending civil liberties, which led to Congress being voted out in [1977](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1977_Indian_general_election).[^cite_note-287] Congress returned to power in 1980. Following a [military operation](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Operation_Blue_Star) at the [Golden Temple](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Golden_Temple) in [Amritsar](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Amritsar), Indira Gandhi was [assassinated](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Assassination_of_Indira_Gandhi) on 31 October 1984.[^cite_note-corbridge-harris-teitelbaum-288] Indira Gandhi's son, [Rajiv Gandhi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rajiv_Gandhi), succeeded her and won the [late 1984 elections](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1984_Indian_general_election).[^cite_note-289] During the [general election](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1991_Indian_general_election) campaign of 1991, Rajiv Gandhi was [assassinated](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Assassination_of_Rajiv_Gandhi) by a [Sri Lankan Tamil](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sri_Lankan_Tamils) [separatist](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Liberation_Tigers_of_Tamil_Eelam). Congress emerged from the elections as the largest party, and [P. V. Narasimha Rao](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/P._V._Narasimha_Rao) formed a [minority government](271434.md) that completed a full five-year term.[^cite_note-corbridge-harris-teitelbaum-288][^cite_note-290] After the [1999](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1999_Indian_general_election) elections, the BJP's [Atal Bihari Vajpayee](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Atal_Bihari_Vajpayee) led a [National Democratic Alliance](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/National_Democratic_Alliance) (NDA) coalition and became the first non-Congress prime minister to complete a five-year term.[^cite_note-hasan-2022-291] The NDA was defeated in [2004](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2004_Indian_general_election) by the Congress-led [United Progressive Alliance](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/United_Progressive_Alliance) (UPA). [Manmohan Singh](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Manmohan_Singh) served as prime minister with external support, returning with increased, independent numbers in [2009](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2009_Indian_general_election).[^cite_note-292] In [2014](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2014_Indian_general_election), the BJP led by [Narendra Modi](444222.md) won an [absolute majority](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Absolute_majority), in the Indian parliament, and expanded it in [2019](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2019_Indian_general_election).[^cite_note-jaffrelot-2019election-293] The party joined an NDA coalition after losing its single-party majority in the [2024 general election](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2024_Indian_general_election). Modi remains the longest-serving non-Congress prime minister.[^cite_note-294]&#x20;



The original preamble of the [Constitution of India](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Constitution_of_India) in 1950. In 1976, during the tenure of Indian Prime Minister [Indira Gandhi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Indira_Gandhi), the first sentence was changed to "Sovereign Socialist Secular Democratic Republic".

The [Constitution of India](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Constitution_of_India) was drafted by the [Constituent Assembly of India](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Constituent_Assembly_of_India) between 1946 and 1949, despite the political disruptions of the [Partition](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Partition_of_India).[^cite_note-FOOTNOTESpear1990242-295] The [Government of India Act 1935](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Government_of_India_Act_1935) was used as a model and framework.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTESpear1990242-295] Long passages from the Act were included. The constitution describes a federal state with a parliamentary system of democracy.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTESpear1990242-295] The federal structure was notable for the strength of the central government, which exclusively controlled defence, foreign affairs, railways, ports, and currency.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTESpear1990242-295] The President, the constitutional head of state, has reserve powers for taking over the administration of a state.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTESpear1990242-295] The central legislature has two houses: the *[Lok Sabha](275456.md)*, whose delegates are directly elected by the people in general elections every five years, and the *[Rajya Sabha](232210.md)*, whose members are nominated by the elected representatives in the states.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTESpear1990242-295] The Constitution also includes features not found in the 1935 Act. The definition of fundamental rights is based on the [Constitution of the United States](31644.md), and the constitutional directives, or goals of endeavour, are based on the [Constitution of Ireland](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Constitution_of_Ireland).[^cite_note-FOOTNOTESpear1990243-296] An Indian institution recommended by the constitution is the *[panchayat](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Panchayati_raj_in_India)* or village committees.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTESpear1990243-296] [Untouchability](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Untouchability) is illegal (Article 17) and caste distinctions are derecognised (Articles 15(2) and 16(2)).[^cite_note-FOOTNOTESpear1990243-296] The promulgation of the Indian Constitution transformed India into a republic within the Commonwealth.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTESpear1990243-296]&#x20;

The [prime minister of India](24452.md) is the [head of government](37570.md) and exercises most [executive power](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Executive_\(government\)).[^cite_note-FOOTNOTESharma1950-297] Appointed by the president,[^cite_note-FOOTNOTESharma2007162-298] the prime minister is supported by the [party](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Political_party) or [political alliance](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Political_alliance) with a majority of seats in the lower house of parliament.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTESharma1950-297] The executive of the Indian government consists of the president, the [vice-president](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Vice_President_of_India), and the [Union Council of Ministers](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Union_Council_of_Ministers)—with the [cabinet](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cabinet_\(government\)) being its executive committee—headed by the prime minister. Any minister holding a portfolio must be a member of one of the houses of parliament.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTESharma200731-299] In the Indian parliamentary system, the executive is subordinate to the legislature; the prime minister and their council are directly responsible to the lower house of the parliament. [Civil servants](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Civil_Services_of_India) act as permanent executives, and all decisions of the executive are implemented by them.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEMathew2003524-300]&#x20;

India has a three-tier unitary [independent judiciary](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Judicial_independence)[^cite_note-FOOTNOTENeuborne2003478-301] comprising the [supreme court](395852.md), headed by the [Chief Justice of India](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Chief_Justice_of_India), 25 [high courts](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/High_courts_of_India), and a large number of trial courts.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTENeuborne2003478-301] The supreme court has [original jurisdiction](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Original_jurisdiction) over cases involving [fundamental rights](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fundamental_rights_in_India) and over disputes between states and the centre and has [appellate jurisdiction](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Appellate_jurisdiction) over the high courts.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTESharma2007238,_255-302] It has the power to both strike down Union or State laws which contravene the Constitution[^cite_note-FOOTNOTESripati1998423–424-303] and invalidate any government action it deems unconstitutional.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEPylee2003b314-304] In recent years, India has experienced [democratic erosion](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Democratic_backsliding), in which a combination of institutional passivity, negligence, and political meddling have crippled the ability of the Supreme Court, the [Election Commission of India](47863083.md), and other federal agencies to restrain an executive branch and ruling party partial to [majoritarian](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Majoritarianism) [Hindu nationalism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hindu_nationalism).[^cite_note-democratic-erosion-211]&#x20;

### Administrative divisions

Main article: [Administrative divisions of India](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Administrative_divisions_of_India)

See also: [Political integration of India](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Political_integration_of_India)



A clickable map of the 28 states and 8 union territories of India

India is a federal union comprising 28 [states](375986.md) and 8 [union territories](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Union_territory).[^cite_note-FOOTNOTELibrary_of_Congress2004-20] All states, as well as the union territories of [Jammu and Kashmir](61449812.md), [Puducherry](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Puducherry_\(union_territory\)) and the [National Capital Territory of Delhi](37756.md), have elected legislatures and governments following the [Westminster system](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Westminster_system). The remaining five union territories are directly ruled by the central government through appointed administrators. In 1956, under the [States Reorganisation Act](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/States_Reorganisation_Act,_1956), states were reorganised on a linguistic basis.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTESharma200749-305] Over a quarter of a million local government bodies operate at the city, town, district, block, and village levels.[^cite_note-306]&#x20;

### Foreign relations

Main article: [Foreign relations of India](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Foreign_relations_of_India)



In the 1950s and 60s, India played a pivotal role in the [Non-Aligned Movement](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Non-Aligned_Movement).

[^cite_note-Dinkel2018-307]

&#x20;From left to right: [Gamal Abdel Nasser](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gamal_Abdel_Nasser) of the [United Arab Republic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/United_Arab_Republic) (now Egypt), [Josip Broz Tito](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Josip_Broz_Tito) of [Yugoslavia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Socialist_Federal_Republic_of_Yugoslavia) and [Jawaharlal Nehru](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jawaharlal_Nehru) in Belgrade, September 1961.

India remained a member of the [Commonwealth of Nations](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Commonwealth_of_Nations) after becoming a republic in 1950.[^cite_note-storm-india-commonwealth-308][^cite_note-309] It strongly supported decolonisation in Africa and Asia in the 1950s, and played a leading role in the Non-Aligned Movement.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTERothermund200048,_227-310] Following a period of cordial relations in the 1950s, India and China went to [war in 1962](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sino-Indian_War), resulting in a decisive military defeat for India.[^cite_note-62-humiliation-311] By 1967, however, India was able to fend off [Chinese excursions into Sikkim](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sino-Indian_War_of_1967).[^cite_note-312]&#x20;

India has had [uneasy relations](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Indo-Pakistani_relations) with its western neighbour, Pakistan. The two went to war in [1947](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Indo-Pakistani_War_of_1947), [1965](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Indo-Pakistani_War_of_1965), [1971](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Indo-Pakistani_War_of_1971), and [1999](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kargil_War). Three wars were fought over the [disputed territory of Kashmir](3030955.md).[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEGilbert2002486–487-313] After the 1965 war, India began to pursue close military and economic [ties with the Soviet Union](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/India-Soviet_Union_relations). By the late 1960s, the Soviet Union was India's largest arms supplier.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTESharma199956-314] China's [nuclear test of 1964](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/596_\(nuclear_test\)) and threats to intervene in support of Pakistan in the 1965 war caused India to produce nuclear weapons.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEPerkovich200160–86,_106–125-315] India conducted its [first nuclear weapons test](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Smiling_Buddha) in 1974 and carried out [additional underground testing](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pokhran-II) in 1998. India has signed neither the [Comprehensive Nuclear-Test-Ban Treaty](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Comprehensive_Nuclear-Test-Ban_Treaty) nor the [Nuclear Non-Proliferation Treaty](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nuclear_Non-Proliferation_Treaty), calling both flawed and discriminatory.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEKumar2010-316] India maintains a "[no first use](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/No_first_use)" nuclear policy and possesses a [nuclear triad](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nuclear_triad) as a part of its "[Minimum Credible Deterrence](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Minimum_Credible_Deterrence)" doctrine.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTENair2007-317][^cite_note-318]&#x20;

Since the end of the [Cold War](325329.md), India has increased its economic, strategic, and military [cooperation with the United States](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/India%E2%80%93United_States_relations) and the [European Union](26411571.md).[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEEuropean_Union_2008-319] In 2008, a [civilian nuclear agreement](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/U.S.%E2%80%93India_Civil_Nuclear_Agreement) was signed between India and the United States. Although India possessed nuclear weapons at the time and was not a party to the Nuclear Non-Proliferation Treaty, it received waivers from the [International Atomic Energy Agency](14984.md) and the [Nuclear Suppliers Group](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nuclear_Suppliers_Group), ending earlier restrictions on India's nuclear technology and commerce; India subsequently signed co-operation agreements involving [civilian nuclear energy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nuclear_power_in_India) with Russia,[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEBritish_Broadcasting_Corporation_2009-320] France,[^cite_note-FOOTNOTERediff_2008_a-321] the [United Kingdom](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/India%E2%80%93United_Kingdom_relations),[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEReuters2010-322] and [Canada](17884694.md).[^cite_note-FOOTNOTECurry2010-323]&#x20;

## Economy

Main article: [Economy of India](1472206.md)

After independence in 1947, India shifted from the colonial economic model to a focus on [industrialisation](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Industrialisation) and [poverty reduction](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Poverty_reduction).[^cite_note-camb-econhist-pp1-2-324] This transition brought a large expansion of government authority. [Government spending](1203070.md) as a share of [gross domestic product](12594.md) (GDP) increased from 3 to 5 per cent in 1931 to 22 per cent by 1981.[^cite_note-camb-econhist-pp1-2-324] [Public administration](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Public_administration) and [state-owned enterprises](204768.md) became major employers.[^cite_note-camb-econhist-pp1-2-324] To manage this [market intervention](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Market_intervention), India developed—during the 1950s—a comprehensive, [federally managed statistical system](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Central_Statistics_Office_\(India\)) to conduct large-scale [sample surveys](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sampling_\(statistics\)) for monitoring and assessing [living standards](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Standard_of_living) or effects of interventions.[^cite_note-camb-econhist-pp1-2-324] The Indian state utilised [fiscal policy](132645.md) and [international assistance](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Aid) to fund [capital-intensive](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Capital_intensity) industrialisation and [subsidise](146719.md) basic [goods](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Good) such as [food](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Food), [fertilisers](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fertiliser), and [electric power](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Electric_power_industry).[^cite_note-camb-econhist-pp3-4-325] However, by the 1970s, this heavy [public spending](1203070.md) became unsustainable.[^cite_note-camb-econhist-pp3-4-325] Domestic [economic growth](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Economic_growth) slowed, leading to a [balance-of-payments crisis](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Currency_crisis), whereas government spending flattened around 28–30 per cent of GDP.[^cite_note-camb-econhist-pp3-4-325] [Government-led planning](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Planning_Commission_\(India\)) yielded mixed results.[^cite_note-camb-econhist-p6-326] Many of India’s big economic changes were not planned by [government bureaucrats](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bureaucrat).[^cite_note-camb-econhist-p6-326] Thus, the [1970s Green Revolution](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Green_Revolution_in_India), mass [labour migration to the Middle East](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Indian_diaspora_in_the_Middle_East), and a [garment-industry boom](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Textile_industry_in_India) all emerged from global market forces and [ non-state actors](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Non-state_actor); the government simply adapted to them after they were underway.[^cite_note-camb-econhist-p6-326]&#x20;

A major shift occurred during the 1990s as India rejoined the global economy.[^cite_note-camb-econhist-p7-8-327][^cite_note-Tomlinson-Reforms1-81] The Indian government reduced [import tariffs](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Import_tariff) and enacted [pro-market reforms](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Economic_liberalization).[^cite_note-camb-econhist-p7-8-327] This phase was initially supported by [worker remittances](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Remittance) from abroad and a sharp rise in exports of [labour-intensive](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Labour_intensity) goods like [textiles](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Textile).[^cite_note-camb-econhist-p7-8-327] Subsequently [service](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tertiary_sector) and [software](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Software) exports accelerated as global [communication](14601.md) costs fell.[^cite_note-camb-econhist-p7-8-327] Overall, private business investment rose significantly as the Indian government shifted its economic priorities.[^cite_note-camb-econhist-p7-8-327] By the mid-2010s, India's [economy](1472206.md) was [rapidly growing](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_countries_by_real_GDP_growth_rate).[^cite_note-Tomlinson-Reforms2-82][^cite_note-camb-econhist-p8-9-328] It led to gains in [poverty reduction](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Poverty_reduction), increased [national tax collection](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Indian_Revenue_Service), and a surge in private investment.[^cite_note-camb-econhist-p8-9-328] As the Indian government methodically withdrew from direct investment in industry, it simultaneously increased public spending on vital [ infrastructure](14602.md) projects to keep pace with accelerating [urbanisation](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Urbanisation_in_India).[^cite_note-camb-econhist-p8-9-328] This economic opening also [diversified](63252200.md) the domestic economy.[^cite_note-camb-econhist-p8-9-328] New industries emerged, most notably in [information technology](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Information_technology_in_India) services;[^cite_note-tomlinson2016-it-growth-210] the national [judicial infrastructure](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Judiciary_of_India) slowly adapted.[^cite_note-camb-econhist-p8-9-328] However, growth was accompanied by widening [income gaps](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Income_gap),[^cite_note-tomlinson-income-gap-84] with some data showing only limited gains in overall [welfare](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Welfare) and everyday [quality of life](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Quality_of_life).[^cite_note-camb-econhist-p8-9-328] Moreover, rapid development has exerted accelerated pressure on the environment. In response, activist political movements emerged to resist [environmental degradation](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Environmental_issues_in_India).[^cite_note-fisher-environ-86][^cite_note-camb-econhist-p8-9-328]&#x20;

### Industries

Main articles: [Industry in India](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Industry_in_India) and [Energy in India](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Energy_in_India)

See also: [Energy policy of India](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Energy_policy_of_India)



An entrance to [Bagmane Tech Park, Bangalore](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bagmane_Group), showing some of the major corporate offices hosted therein

India is a worldwide centre for [information technology](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Information_technology) and [business services](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Business_process_management), which add 7.5% to GDP and over 35% to [services sold abroad](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Service_export).[^cite_note-PIBServices2026-331][^cite_note-IBEF_IT2026-332] This sector is sustained by a large English-speaking skilled labour force, with revenues surpassing \$300 billion in Fiscal Year 2026.[^cite_note-NasscomStrategic2026-333] Although the industry once relied on basic [call centres](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Call_centre) and simple software fixes, it has shifted toward [cloud computing](19541494.md), [artificial intelligence](1164.md), and international [tech hubs](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tech_hub).[^cite_note-DCosta2025-334][^cite_note-Gaudecker2025-335] Bangalore has grown from a cheap offshore site into a top centre for corporate research, making it one of the largest technology hubs outside Silicon Valley.[^cite_note-336][^cite_note-337]&#x20;

India's automotive sector comprises 7.1% of its economy.[^cite_note-pib-automobiles-2025-338] It produces 28 million vehicles every year[^cite_note-pib-automobiles-2025-338] of which the vast majority are two-wheelers (77%) followed by passenger cars (18%).[^cite_note-siam-auto2025-339] In 2022, 8 per cent of Indian households owned a car, 55 per cent owned a bicycle, and 54 per cent a motor cycle or scooter.[^cite_note-rampal-2022-340] In 2023, India became the third-largest automobile market in the world, with buyers, many of whom are in their 30s, increasingly preferring SUVs.[^cite_note-shah-2026-reuters-341]&#x20;

India's pharmaceutical sector relies on a vast network of production facilities focused on low-cost manufacturing. Thousands of individual factories and domestic firms comprise this sector and together make India the main worldwide source of generic medications and vaccines by volume.[^cite_note-IBEF-Pharma-2026-342] However, the sector faces supply line vulnerabilities, especially a reliance on China for over 70% of its raw [active pharmaceutical ingredients](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Active_ingredient) (APIs).[^cite_note-shah-2025-343] Although India faces international quality-control audits in its factories, adverse outcomes have dropped from 12 per cent in 2015 to 8 per cent in 2025, and 80% of those were in small firms not the top 20 companies.[^cite_note-PharmaAudits-2026-344]&#x20;

India's total installed electricity capacity exceeds 532 GW, with non-fossil and renewable energy sources contributing more than 283.5 GW, or roughly 53%.[^cite_note-power-sector-2026-345] [The country's usage of coal](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Coal_in_India) is a major cause of [India's greenhouse gas emissions](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Climate_change_in_India#Greenhouse_gas_emissions), but [its renewable energy capacity](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Renewable_energy_in_India) is growing.[^cite_note-346] India emits about 7% of global greenhouse gas emissions. This equates to about 2.5 tonnes of [carbon dioxide](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Carbon_dioxide) per person per year, which is half the world average.[^cite_note-USAID2018-347][^cite_note-UNEP2019-348] Increasing [access to electricity](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Electrification) and [clean cooking](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Clean_cooking) with [liquefied petroleum gas](361384.md) have been priorities for energy in India.[^cite_note-349]&#x20;

## Demographics

Main article: [Demographics of India](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Demographics_of_India)

India's population was 361 million in 1951.[^cite_note-Dyson2018-172-78] It grew to approximately 1.464 billion in 2025,[^cite_note-unfpa-in-2025-79] surpassing China's population in 2021.[^cite_note-poston-sullivan-2026-31] 1,210,193,422 residents were reported in the [2011 provisional census report](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2011_Census_of_India).[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEProvisional_Population_Totals_Paper_1_of_2011_India160-353] The median age was 28.7 in 2020.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTECentral_Intelligence_Agency-354] Medical advances made in the last 50 years as well as increased agricultural productivity brought about by the "[Green Revolution](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Green_Revolution_in_India)" have caused India's population to grow rapidly,[^cite_note-FOOTNOTERorabacher201035–39-355] though India's decennial rates of growth are decreasing: its population grew by 17.64% from 2001 to 2011,[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEProvisional_Population_Totals_Paper_1_of_2011_India165-356] compared to 21.54% growth in the previous decade (1991–2001).[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEProvisional_Population_Totals_Paper_1_of_2011_India165-356] The first post-colonial census, conducted in 1951, counted 361 million people.[^cite_note-357] The [life expectancy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Life_expectancy) at birth has increased from 49.7 years in 1970–1975 to 72.0 years in 2023.[^cite_note-\:5-358][^cite_note-359] The under-five [mortality](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Infant_mortality) rate for the country was 113 per 1,000 live births in 1994, and had declined to 41.1 per 1,000 live births by 2018.[^cite_note-\:5-358]&#x20;

The 2011 census reported a human sex ratio of 940 females per 1,000 males.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEProvisional_Population_Totals_Paper_1_of_2011_India160-353] [Female infanticide in India](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Female_infanticide_in_India), and lately [female foeticide](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Female_foeticide_in_India), have created lopsided gender ratios; the number of [missing women](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Missing_women) in the country quadrupled from 15 million to 63 million during the period 1964–2014, faster than the population growth during the same period.[^cite_note-The_Hindu_November_17_2019c-360] According to an Indian government study, an additional 21 million girls are unwanted and do not receive adequate care.[^cite_note-The_Guardian_November_17_2019c-361] Despite a government ban on sex-selective foeticide, the practice persists.[^cite_note-Foreign_Policy_November_17_2019c-362]&#x20;

Migration from rural to urban areas has been an important dynamic in India's recent history. The number of people living in urban areas grew by 31.2% between 1991 and 2001.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEGarg2005-363] In 2001, over 70% lived in rural areas.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEDysonVisaria2005115–129-364][^cite_note-FOOTNOTERatna2007271–272-365] The level of urbanisation increased further from 27.81% in the 2001 census to 31.16% in the 2011 census. The overall population growth rate slowed because rural growth declined sharply after 1991.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEChandramouli2011-366] In the 2011 census, there were 53 [million-plus urban agglomerations in India](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_million-plus_urban_agglomerations_in_India), among them [Mumbai](19189.md), [Delhi](37756.md), [Kolkata](47905.md), [Chennai](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Chennai), [Bangalore](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bangalore), [Hyderabad](37534.md) and [Ahmedabad](206337.md), in decreasing order by population.[^cite_note-censusindia_2011-367]&#x20;

In the 1980s, when the first multinational corporations began to set up centres in India, they chose Bangalore because of the large pool of skilled graduates in the area, in turn due to the many science and engineering colleges in the surrounding region.\[331]

### Languages

Main article: [Languages of India](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Languages_of_India)

Languages of India belong to several [language families](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Language_family). The [2011 Census of India](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2011_Census_of_India), the last conducted by the Indian government, gives the following breakdown:[^cite_note-jolad-agarwal-369]&#x20;

| Language families and speakers in India[^cite_note-jolad-agarwal-369] |  |  |  |  |  |
| --- | --- | --- | --- | --- | --- |

| [Iranian](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Iranian_languages) | 1 | 21,677 | 0%[^cite_note-370] |  |  |

There are also small numbers of speakers of [Tai–Kadai](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kra%E2%80%93Dai_languages), [Andamanese](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Andamanese_languages), and minor language families and [isolates](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Language_isolate).[^cite_note-Moseley2008-371]<sup>: 283 </sup>&#x20;



On the reverse of each of India's [paper money notes](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Paper_money), the denomination is listed in a panel on the left in 15 languages, in addition to Hindi and English, which appear more prominently elsewhere. These are from top to bottom: 1. Assamese, 2. Bengali, 3. Gujarati, 4. Kannada, 5. Kashmiri, 6. Konkani, 7. Malayalam, 8. Marathi, 9. Nepali, 10. Oriya, 11. Punjabi, 12. Sanskrit, 13. Tamil, 14. Telugu, 15. Urdu.

[^cite_note-rupee-15lang-372]

The [Constituent Assembly of India](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Constituent_Assembly_of_India) chose the official language of India's federal government in September 1949 after three years of debate between two opposing camps. [Hindi language](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hindi_language) protagonists wanted Hindi in the [Devanagari script](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Devanagari_script) to be the sole "national language" of India whereas delegates from South India preferred English to have a place in the Constitution.[^cite_note-lerner-Hindi-373][^cite_note-isaka-Hindi-374] The compromise reached declared (i) Hindi to be the "official language" of India's federal government; (ii) English to be an associate official language for 15 years during which Hindi's formal lexicon would be developed; and (iii) the international form of [Hindu–Arabic numerals](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hindu%E2%80%93Arabic_numeral_system) to be the official numerals.[^cite_note-lerner-Hindi-373][^cite_note-isaka-Hindi-374] The compromise resolution became articles 343–351 of India's constitution, which came into effect on 26 January 1950.[^cite_note-lerner-Hindi-373][^cite_note-isaka-Hindi-374] In 1965, after bitter opposition from [South India](678583.md) to Hindi becoming the sole official language, a compromise was reached whereby English would continue to be an "associate official language" indefinitely.[^cite_note-375][^cite_note-376]&#x20;

The [Eighth Schedule of India's Constitution](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Eighth_Schedule_to_the_Constitution_of_India) also recognises 22 languages, including Hindi but not English, which the government must develop. These are sometimes called "scheduled languages". This list includes major regional languages, but also others—such as [Sanskrit](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sanskrit), which no longer has [first language](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/First_language) speakers in India, and [Urdu](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Urdu), which is not region-specific—because of their value to India's cultural heritage.[^cite_note-KachruKachru2008-1-377][^cite_note-GazzolaWickström2016-378][^cite_note-Groff2017-lead-379] In 1950, there were 14 scheduled languages: [Assamese](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Assamese_language), [Bengali](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bengali_language), [Gujarati](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gujarati_language), [Hindi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hindi), [Kannada](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kannada), [Kashmiri](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kashmiri_language), [Malayalam](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Malayalam), [Marathi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Marathi_language), [Oriya](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Odia_language), [Punjabi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Punjabi_language), Sanskrit, [Tamil](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tamil_language), [Telugu](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Telugu_language) and Urdu.[^cite_note-jolad-agarwal-369] In the following decades constitutional amendments added others: [Sindhi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sindhi_language) (1967), [Nepali](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nepali_language), [Manipuri](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Meitei_language), and [Konkani](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Konkani_language) (1992), [Maithili](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Maithili_language), [Dogri](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dogri_language), [Santali](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Santali_language) and [Bodo](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Boro_language_\(India\)) (2004).[^cite_note-jolad-agarwal-369]&#x20;

### Religion

Main article: [Religion in India](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Religion_in_India)

Hinduism is the majority faith followed by nearly 80% of the population; Islam is the largest minority at over 14%.[^cite_note-acharya2022-380] India also has populations of [Christians](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Christianity_in_India) and [Sikhs](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sikhs) in the tens of millions, [Buddhists](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Buddhists) and [Jains](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jains) fewer than 10 million, and historical populations of [Zoroastrians](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Parsis) in the tens of thousands and [Jews](25955086.md) less than ten thousand.&#x20;

As of the 2011 census, India's last census, Hindus comprised a majority in 28 of India’s 35 states and union territories; this included the four most populous states: Uttar Pradesh (total population: 200 million; Hindu population: 80% of the state's total), Maharashtra (112 million, 80%), Bihar (104 million, 83%) and West Bengal (91 million, 71%).[^cite_note-kramer-statewise-religions-381] Muslims are in the majority in [Lakshadweep Islands](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lakshadweep) (60,000, 97%) and [Jammu and Kashmir](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jammu_and_Kashmir_\(state\)) (8.6 million, 68%).[^cite_note-kramer-statewise-religions-381] Indian Christians make up about 2.3% of India's population.[^cite_note-christianity2.3percent-382] [Saint Thomas Christians](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Saint_Thomas_Christians) number around 8.7 million, whereas [Indian Catholics](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Catholicism_in_India) number 11.8 million.[^cite_note-thomas-numbers-383] Nearly 33% of [Adivasi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Adivasi) (or [Scheduled Tribes](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Scheduled_Tribe) of India's Constitution) are Christian. They are concentrated in three hill states of the northeast, [Mizoram](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mizoram), [Meghalaya](326019.md), and [Nagaland](408677.md), where they constitute between 70% and 90% of the population.[^cite_note-northeast-christians-384] As per the 2011 census, Sikhs comprised less than 2% of India's population, though they were a majority in Punjab state, where more than 87% of Sikhs were farmers.[^cite_note-oxford-sikhs-post47-385] Indian Buddhists number 8.45 million, with 77%, or 6.5 million, living in the state of Maharashtra.[^cite_note-kramer-statewise-religions-381] Indian Jains number 4.45 million, and Zoroastrians (Parsis), 60,000.[^cite_note-kramer-pop-growth-386] As of 2019, there were 3,000 [Jews](25955086.md) in India, down from 28,000 in 1950.[^cite_note-weil-2019-387] Other religions in India (and population in the 2011 census) are [Sarnaism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sarnaism) (4.96 million) and [Gondi-Koyapunem](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gondi_people#Koyapunem) (1.03 million) and [Sari dharma](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Santal_people#Religion) (510,000).[^cite_note-kramer-pop-growth-386]&#x20;

In 1951—India's first postcolonial census—the percentage breakdown of India's religions was: Hindu (84.1%), Muslims (9.8%), Christians (2.3%), Sikhs (1.9%), Buddhists (0.7%) and Jains (0.5%); sixty years later, in India's last census (2011), the percentages were: Hindus (79.8%), Muslims (14.2%), Christians (2.3%), Sikhs (1.7%), Buddhists (0.7%) and Jains (0.4%)[^cite_note-kramer-religious-share-change-388] In absolute numbers, during the period 1951–2011, India's religious groups grew in the following manner: Hindus (304 million to 966 million), Muslims (35 million to 172 million), Christians (8.3 million to 28 million), Sikhs (6.82 million to 20.83 million), Buddhists (2.67 million to 8.44 million) and Jains (1.66 million to 4.45 million).[^cite_note-kramer-pop-growth-386] In the decade 1951–1961, the population growth by religions was: Hindus (20.7%), Muslims (32.7%), Christians (29%), Sikhs (10.3%), Buddhists (5.9%), Jains (3.7%); in the decade 2001–2011, the growth was: Hindus (16.7%), Muslims (24.7%), Christians (15.7%), Sikhs (16.1%), Buddhists (4.8%), and Jains (2.2%). All religions have registered declining growth rates.[^cite_note-kramer-religious-share-change-388]&#x20;

[Birth rates](515898.md), [population growth](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Population_growth), and access to education influence how communities mobilise politically to preserve their [places of worship](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Place_of_worship), to manage [charitable trusts](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Charitable_trust), and to protect their [family laws](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Family_law).[^cite_note-PewReligiousChange2021-389] Differences in states' laws, especially those prohibiting [religious conversion](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Religious_conversion)—usually from Hinduism to other faiths—colour the daily lives of minority communities.[^cite_note-USCIRF2023-390] Providing [constitutional protections](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Constitutionalism) for [minorities](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Minorities) in the face of [majoritarian](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Majoritarian) pressure remains a fraught challenge.[^cite_note-Bhargava2008-391] India is a [secular state](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Secular_state) and guarantees the [fundamental right](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fundamental_right) to [freedom of religion](46006.md)[^cite_note-Smith1963-392] ("Liberty of thought, expression, belief, faith, and worship").[^cite_note-IndiaConstPreamble-393]&#x20;

### Education

Main article: [Education in India](293121.md)

See also: [Literacy in India](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Literacy_in_India) and [History of education in the Indian subcontinent](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/History_of_education_in_the_Indian_subcontinent)

In 1911, there were three primary schools for every ten villages in [British India](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/British_India).[^cite_note-394] Early literacy was low: It was 5% in 1891 (9% for men and 1% for women) and 7% in 1921 (12% for men and 2% for women). Historically, local diversity limited literacy growth in India because caste and religious fragmentation reduced private spending.[^cite_note-pathania-395][^cite_note-dandapani-396] Literacy rates rose to 18% in 1951 (27% for men and 9% for women) and 41% in 1981 (53% for men and 29% for women).[^cite_note-pathania-395][^cite_note-dandapani-396] By 2011, literacy reached 74.04% (82.14% for men and 65.46% for women). They ranged from Kerala's 93.91% to Bihar's 63.82%. Meanwhile, rural gains reached twice the level of urban, narrowing the rural-urban gap from 21.2 percentage points in 2001 to 16.1 in 2011.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEProvisional_Population_Totals_Paper_1_of_2011_India163-397]&#x20;

The central, state, and local governments manage education in India.[^cite_note-NITIAayog-Education-2026-398] Every child between the ages of 6 and 14 has a right to education, which is free and compulsory.[^cite_note-RTE-Act-2009-399] Traditionally, children had spent eight years in primary school before moving on to high school.[^cite_note-NEP-2020-400] In 2020, the [National Education Policy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/National_Education_Policy_2020) changed this division to one based on developmental stages: a foundational stage: five years of pre-school and [standards](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Educational_stage) 1–2, for ages 3–8; a preparatory stage: standards 3–5; a middle stage: standards 6–8; and a secondary stage: standards 9–12.[^cite_note-NEP-2020-400] Critics argue that the new plan, although progressive, suffers from public underfunding and inadequate school infrastructure.[^cite_note-jha-parvati-2-401]&#x20;

The first universities in India were founded in Calcutta, Bombay, and Madras in 1857, the last year of [East India Company rule](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Company_rule_in_India).[^cite_note-m\&m12-universities-402] India now has one of the world's largest post-secondary education systems.[^cite_note-AISHE-2024-403] Recent college reforms envisage offering more flexible degrees, creating online learning tools, and inviting foreign universities to set up campuses in India.[^cite_note-NEP-2020-400] However, large gaps remain between rural and urban areas.[^cite_note-SAGE-EduInequality-2024-404] School quality, campus building conditions, and computer access vary substantially based on a student's gender and family's wealth.[^cite_note-Frontiers-EduGaps-2022-405] Poor job prospects for graduates and uneven rule enforcement across different states have faced criticism from researchers and the public alike.[^cite_note-Agrawal-IJED-EduGaps-406]&#x20;

### Health

Main article: [Health in India](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Health_in_India)



The [Anganwadi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Anganwadi) centre in Ada village, [Asifabad district](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Asifabad_district), [Telangana](990267.md) in July 2022. [Child undernutrition](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Undernutrition_in_children) and [stunting](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Stunted_growth) rates have steadily declined in India, though the rate has slowed over the last decade.

[^cite_note-Kavishwar2025-410]

[^cite_note-Rao2023-411]

&#x20;Anganwadi centres play a central role by providing localised supplementary nutrition and health counselling.

[^cite_note-Mondal2026-412]

[^cite_note-Das2024-413]

Since its 1947 independence, India has significantly reduced historically [endemic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Endemic) diseases, though some remain intractable. The country was certified smallpox-free in April 1977 following two years of surveillance after its last *[Variola major](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Variola_major)* case.[^cite_note-bhattach-2004-414][^cite_note-bhatt-dasgupt-2009-415] It was officially certified polio-free by the [World Health Organisation](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/World_Health_Organisation) on 27 March 2014, after successfully completing three consecutive years without recorded cases of [wild poliovirus](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Wild_poliovirus).[^cite_note-john-vashishtha-416][^cite_note-kloeger-etal-417] In contrast, cholera remains endemic to [Ganges-Meghna Delta](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ganges_Delta) [estuaries](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Estuary),[^cite_note-Alam2007PNAS-418] resulting in seasonal outbreaks caused by monsoons and [water supply network](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Water_supply_network) shortcomings.[^cite_note-kanungo-etal-2021-419][^cite_note-tantadas-etal-2022-420] [Tuberculosis](30653.md) is a major concern; India bears a quarter of the global tuberculosis [disease burden](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Disease_burden).[^cite_note-WHO_TB_Report_2025-421][^cite_note-khatri-etal-critical-perspectives-422] Historical [malaria](20423.md) cases have dropped from millions to hundreds of thousands annually, but India experiences localised seasonal increases caused by [drug-resistant](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Drug_resistance) variants of the *[Plasmodium falciparum](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Plasmodium_falciparum)* and *[Plasmodium vivax](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Plasmodium_vivax)* [vectors](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Disease_vector).[^cite_note-bhowmick-malaria-423]&#x20;

[Patients](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Patient) in India often face a stark choice: use basic, free, [publicly funded health care](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Publicly_funded_health_care) or pay high [out-of-pocket fees](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Out-of-pocket_expense) for better-equipped care from private doctors and hospitals.[^cite_note-Kumar2024-424] This divide is particularly severe outside cities, as public clinics in rural areas rarely have enough doctors or nurses to meet local demands.[^cite_note-thiag-2021-425] When serious medical emergencies occur, poorer families are often forced to sell land or take out high-interest loans to pay for private care.[^cite_note-thomas-sahu-dash-2025-426] This resulting medical debt pushes approximately 55 million people into poverty every year.[^cite_note-Soni2026-427]&#x20;

Long-term exposure to outdoor and indoor [air pollution](10934212.md) adds a major environmental risk to [public health](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Public_health) in India.[^cite_note-Saraf-etal-LancetPlanetary2024-428] [Particulate matter](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Particulate_matter) emissions from [vehicles](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Vehicle) and [factories](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Factory) are linked to a rise in [chronic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Chronic_condition) [respiratory diseases](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Respiratory_disease), [strokes](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Stroke), and [cardiovascular disease](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cardiovascular_disease), together adding an estimated 1.7 million [premature deaths](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Death#premature_death) annually.[^cite_note-LancetCountdown2025-429] India's early [COVID-19 lockdown](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/COVID-19_lockdowns) bought time to rapidly increase local testing and protective gear production; however, later waves of the virus overwhelmed the healthcare system, leading to severe shortages of oxygen and intensive care beds.[^cite_note-Kaur-COVID-2023-430][^cite_note-LancetIndia2026-431] Post-[pandemic](62750956.md) [epidemiological studies](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Epidemiology#Types_of_studies) estimate that cumulative [excess deaths](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Excess_death) in India reached between 3.2[^cite_note-Jha2022-432] and 4.7 million.[^cite_note-who-covid-india-2023-433]&#x20;

## Culture

Main article: [Culture of India](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Culture_of_India)

### Society

Main articles: [Caste system in India](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Caste_system_in_India), [Gender inequality in India](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gender_inequality_in_India), and [Islam in India](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Islam_in_India)

Although sometimes applied to other cultures and religions, [caste](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Caste) is a fundamental [social institution](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Social_institution) of India, most fully developed among [Hindus](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hindus).[^cite_note-béteille-caste-434][^cite_note-436] All Hindus fall broadly into four castes, or *[varnas](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Varna_\(Hinduism\))*: [Brahmin](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Brahmin), or priests, at the top; below them [Kshatriya](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kshatriya), or warriors; further below, [Vaishya](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Vaishya), or merchants and farmers; and at the bottom, [Shudra](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Shudra), or the service class. Outside the caste system, and of traditional Hinduism, lie people formerly called "outcastes" or "[untouchables](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Untouchability)," and now [scheduled caste](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Scheduled_caste) (a term used in India's constitution) or [Dalit](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dalit), a later self-description of [pride](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pride), meaning "broken" or "downtrodden". Each caste is further divided into sub-castes, or [jātis](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/J%C4%81ti), many of which are tied to occupations. However, the custom of [endogamy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Endogamy), or marrying within one's subcaste, makes caste a [hereditary](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hereditary) label rather than one of [occupational choice](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Occupational_choice_model) and has entrenched the caste system.[^cite_note-atrey1-437] The [Constituent Assembly of India](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Constituent_Assembly_of_India) abolished untouchability in 1947,[^cite_note-nyt-india-constit-assmb-caste-438] the Republic of India did more formally in 1950, and India has since enacted other [anti-discrimination laws](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Anti-discrimination_law) and social welfare initiatives related to caste. Still, caste-based inequality, discrimination, segregation, and [violence](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Caste-related_violence_in_India) persist.[^cite_note-439][^cite_note-440]&#x20;

Multi-generational [patrilin](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Patrilineality)eal [joint families](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Joint_family) have been the norm in India, though [nuclear families](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nuclear_family) are becoming common in [urban areas](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Urbanisation_in_India).[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEMakar2007-441] A very large majority of Indians have [their marriages arranged](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Arranged_marriage_in_the_Indian_subcontinent) by their parents or family elders.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEMedora2003-442] Marriage is thought to be for life;[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEMedora2003-442] and the [divorce rate](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Divorce_rate) is extremely low,[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEJonesRamdas2005111-443] less than one in a thousand.[^cite_note-444] Many women marry before reaching 18, which is their legal [marriageable age](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Marriageable_age); [child marriages](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Child_marriage_in_India) are not uncommon, especially in rural areas.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTECullen-Dupont200996-445] In large parts of Hindu [northern India](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Northern_India), moreover, a form of territorial [exogamy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Exogamy) is observed in which a bride marries out of her natal village, and her parents do not visit her in her married home; the annual rite [raksha bandhan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Raksha_bandhan), during which married women return to their natal homes, has served both to affirm [bonds](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Human_bonding) with their natal families and offer a recourse in times of marital stress.[^cite_note-Agarwal1994-446][^cite_note-Coleman2017-447]&#x20;

Modern [Indian Muslim](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Indian_Muslim) society is highly diverse, divided by [local languages](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Languages_of_India), distinct cultural backgrounds, and internal [social classes](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Social_class).[^cite_note-assadi-zaat-448] However, Indian Muslims have much lower college enrollment, less access to [banking](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Banking_in_India) services, and far fewer [formal jobs](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Formal_employment) than average.[^cite_note-jaff-kalai-449] Additionally, due to safety worries and unfair housing practices, many urban Muslims have been forced to move into separated neighbourhoods that often lack basic [public services](1291782.md).[^cite_note-parwez-ghettoization-450] Still, Muslim families regularly see higher rates of [child survival](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Child_survival) compared to other [religious](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Religion_in_India) and social groups.[^cite_note-bhalotra-child-451] The [Saint Thomas Christians](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Saint_Thomas_Christians), also known as Syrian Christians, trace their history to the arrival ashore in [South India](678583.md) of St [Thomas the Apostle](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Thomas_the_Apostle) in the 1st century CE,[^cite_note-rose-2024-452] though inscriptions place their origins to the mid-first-millennium CE;[^cite_note-thomas-christian-inscription-453] they spread along the [Malabar Coast](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Malabar_Coast) of the state of [Kerala](4349459.md),[^cite_note-thomas-kerala-454] and have their distinctive [liturgical traditions](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Liturgy).[^cite_note-rose-2024-452] [Indian Catholics](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Catholic_Church_in_India) date their presence to 1500 soon after the arrival of [Vasco da Gama](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Vasco_da_Gama) in [Calicut](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Calicut). [Sikhism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sikhism_in_India) is a monotheistic tradition founded by [Guru Nanak](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Guru_Nanak) in the late 15th-century [Punjab](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Punjab); it grew into a tight-knit fellowship under later [gurus](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sikh_gurus), the last guru ceding authority to the holy book [Guru Granth Sahib](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Guru_Granth_Sahib).[^cite_note-455]&#x20;

[Buddhism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/History_of_Buddhism_in_India), founded by [the Buddha](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/The_Buddha) in the mid-5th century BCE in the middle [Ganges plain](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ganges_plain), eventually spread beyond India to [Southeast Asia](28741.md) and [East Asia](19605700.md), even as it waned in its homeland; nevertheless, its [statues](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Buddha_statues) and [stupas](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Stupa)—stone realisations of the Buddha—remain prominent across [the subcontinent](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Indian_subcontinent), drawing [pilgrims](23763.md) from around the world.[^cite_note-456] [Jainism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jainism_in_India), anciently contemporaneous with Buddhism, has two main sects, [Śvetāmbara](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/%C5%9Avet%C4%81mbara), whose monks and nuns wear robes and use bowls to receive [alms](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Alms), and [Digambara](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Digambara), whose monks go naked and cup their hands for alms.[^cite_note-jain-sects-457] Jains today, regardless of sect, have aligned themselves with [environmentalism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Environmentalism).[^cite_note-jain-environmentalism-458] The [Parsis](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Parsis) follow [Zoroastrianism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Zoroastrianism_in_India); they arrived on [India's western coast](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Coastal_South_West_India) from [Iran](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Zoroastrianism_in_Iran) late in the first millennium CE and rose in trade and industry, becoming early leaders of [Indian nationalism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Indian_nationalism). The [Qissa-i Sanjan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Qissa-i_Sanjan) records their legendary arrival and welcome in India.[^cite_note-459] [Judaism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/History_of_the_Jews_in_India), which had become established in India by the 8th-century CE, was never subject to [antisemitism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Antisemitism_in_India) from Hindus, Christians, or Parsis, nor to [ethnic tensions](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ethnic_tensions) from [Indian Muslims](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Indian_Muslims); it has preserved distinctive Indo-Judaic customs.[^cite_note-weil-2019-387][^cite_note-460]&#x20;

India has had a long history of [religious pluralism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Religious_pluralism) that shapes its modern society.[^cite_note-chana-lobo-pluralism-461][^cite_note-464] A large majority of Indian Muslims, [Christians](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Christianity_in_India), and Sikhs participate in customs, or adhere to beliefs, that were once unique to Hinduism (e.g. *[karma](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Karma)*).[^cite_note-PewIndia2021-465] Muslims, India's largest religious minority, have contributed significantly to a synthetic Indo-Islamic religious culture.[^cite_note-indo-islamic-religion-466] [Sufism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sufism_in_India), the heterodox mystical movement in Islam found receptiveness among adherents of the medieval regional [Shaivite](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Shaivism) and [Vaishnavite](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Vaishnavism) traditions of [Bhakti](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bhakti).[^cite_note-heterodoxy-bhakti-muslim-467][^cite_note-469] Sikh [gurdwaras](482849.md) and Sufi [dargahs](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dargah) often attract both visitors and worshippers from other religions.[^cite_note-PewIndia2021-465] India has monastic traditions such as that of [Jain ascetics](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jain_ascetics) some of whom practice an extreme form of [nonviolence](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nonviolence) (*[ahimsa](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ahimsa)*) and Buddhist [communities](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sangha) that have [preserved ancient styles](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lineage_\(Buddhism\)) of [meditations](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Buddhist_meditation).[^cite_note-Dundas2002-470] This religious diversity coexists with religious [endogamy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Endogamy)—people rarely marry outside their faith. There is also [religious segregation](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Religious_segregation) in housing—religious groups cluster by choice or necessity. Thus, India's religious picture has both rich intermixing and clear divisions.[^cite_note-PewIndia2021-465]&#x20;

### Visual art

Main articles: [Indian art](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Indian_art), [Indian painting](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Indian_painting), and [Architecture of India](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Architecture_of_India)

India has a very ancient tradition of art, which has exchanged many influences with the rest of [Eurasia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Eurasia), especially in the first millennium. During this period [Buddhist art](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Buddhist_art) spread with Indian religions to [Central](6742.md), [East](19605700.md) and [Southeast Asia](28741.md), the last also greatly influenced by Hindu art.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTERowland1970185–198,_252,_385–466-477] Thousands of [seals from the Indus Valley civilisation](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Indus_Valley_civilisation#Seals) of the third millennium BCE have been found, usually carved with animals, but also some with human figures. The [Pashupati seal](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pashupati_seal), excavated in [Mohenjo-daro](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mohenjo-daro), Pakistan, in 1928–29, is the best known.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTECraven199714–16-478][^cite_note-FOOTNOTEHarle199417–18-479] Virtually no art survives from a long period following the Indus Valley Civilisation.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEHarle199417–18-479][^cite_note-FOOTNOTERowland197046–47-480] Almost all surviving ancient Indian art thereafter is in various forms of religious [sculpture](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Indian_sculpture) in durable materials, or coins. There was probably originally far more in wood, which is lost. In north India [Mauryan art](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mauryan_art) is the first imperial movement.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTECraven199735–46-481][^cite_note-FOOTNOTERowland197067–70-482][^cite_note-FOOTNOTEHarle199422–24-483]&#x20;

Over the following centuries a distinctly Indian style of sculpting the human figure developed, with less interest in articulating precise anatomy than [ancient Greek sculpture](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ancient_Greek_sculpture) but showing smoothly flowing forms expressing *prana* ("breath" or life-force).[^cite_note-FOOTNOTECraven199722,_88-484][^cite_note-FOOTNOTERowland197035,_99–100-485] This is often complicated by the need to give figures multiple arms or heads, or represent different genders on the left and right of figures, as with the [Ardhanarishvara](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ardhanarishvara) form of Shiva and [Parvati](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Parvati).[^cite_note-FOOTNOTECraven199718–19-486][^cite_note-FOOTNOTEBlurton1993151-487]&#x20;

Most of the earliest large sculpture is Buddhist, either excavated from Buddhist [stupas](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Stupa) such as [Sanchi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sanchi), [Sarnath](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sarnath) and [Amaravati](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Amaravati_Stupa),[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEHarle199432–38-488] or is rock cut [reliefs](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Relief) at sites such as [Ajanta](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ajanta_Caves), [Karla](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Karla_Caves) and [Ellora](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ellora). Hindu and Jain sites appear rather later.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEHarle199443–55-489][^cite_note-FOOTNOTERowland1970113–119-490] In spite of this complex mixture of religious traditions, generally, the prevailing artistic style at any time and place has been shared by the major religious groups, and sculptors probably usually served all communities.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEBlurton199310–11-491] [Gupta art](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gupta_art), at its peak c. 300 CE – c. 500 CE, is often regarded as a classical period whose influence lingered for many centuries after; it saw a new dominance of Hindu sculpture, as at the [Elephanta Caves](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Elephanta_Caves).[^cite_note-FOOTNOTECraven1997111–121-492][^cite_note-FOOTNOTEMichell200044–70-493] Across the north, this became rather stiff and formulaic after c. 800 CE, though rich with finely carved detail in the surrounds of statues.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEHarle1994212–216-494] But in the South, under the [Pallava](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pallava_dynasty) and [Chola dynasties](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Chola_dynasty), sculpture in both stone and bronze had a [sustained period of great achievement](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Chola_art_and_architecture#Sculpture_and_bronzes); the large bronzes with Shiva as [Nataraja](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nataraja) have become an iconic symbol of India.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTECraven1997152–160-495][^cite_note-FOOTNOTEBlurton1993225–227-496]&#x20;

Ancient paintings have only survived at a few sites, of which the crowded scenes of court life in the [Ajanta Caves](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ajanta_Caves) are some of the most important.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEHarle1994356–361-497][^cite_note-FOOTNOTERowland1970242–251-498] Painted manuscripts of religious texts survive from Eastern India from 10th century onwards, most of the earliest being Buddhist and later Jain. These significantly influenced later artistic styles.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEHarle1994361–370-499] The Persian-derived [Deccan painting](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Deccan_painting), starting just before the [Mughal miniature](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mughal_miniature), between them give the first large body of secular painting, with an emphasis on portraits, and the recording of princely pleasures and wars.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTECraven1997202–208-500][^cite_note-FOOTNOTEHarle1994372–382,_400–406-501] The style spread to Hindu courts, especially [among the Rajputs](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rajput_painting), and developed a variety of styles, with the smaller courts often the most innovative, with figures such as [Nihâl Chand](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nih%C3%A2l_Chand) and [Nainsukh](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nainsukh).[^cite_note-FOOTNOTECraven1997222–243-502][^cite_note-FOOTNOTEHarle1994384–397,_407–420-503] As a market developed among European residents, it was supplied by [Company painting](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Company_painting) by Indian artists with considerable Western influence.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTECraven1997243-504][^cite_note-FOOTNOTEMichell2000210-505] In the 19th century, cheap [Kalighat paintings](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kalighat_painting) of gods and everyday life, done on paper, were urban [folk art](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Folk_art) from [Calcutta](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Calcutta), which later saw the [Bengal School of Art](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bengal_School_of_Art), reflecting the art colleges founded by the British, the first movement in [modern Indian painting](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Modern_Indian_painting).[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEMichell2000210–211-506][^cite_note-FOOTNOTEBlurton1993211-507]&#x20;
