### Mathematics

Main articles: [Indian mathematics](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Indian_mathematics) and [Kerala school of astronomy and mathematics](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kerala_school_of_astronomy_and_mathematics)

Significant mathematics began in India in the first millennium BCE. The *[Śulba Sūtras](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Shulba_Sutras)* (literally, "Aphorisms of the Chords" in [Vedic Sanskrit](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Vedic_Sanskrit)) (c. 700–400 BCE) contain the earliest extant verbal expression of the [Pythagorean theorem](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pythagorean_theorem) (although very likely it had been known to the [Old Babylonians](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/First_Babylonian_dynasty).)[^cite_note-hayashi2005-p363-508][^cite_note-511] All mathematical works were orally transmitted until approximately 500 BCE; thereafter, they were transmitted both orally and in manuscript form. The oldest extant mathematical document produced on the Indian subcontinent is the birch bark [Bakhshali manuscript](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bakhshali_manuscript) from the 7th century CE.[^cite_note-hayashi95-512][^cite_note-plofker-brit6-513]&#x20;

During the classical period of Indian mathematics (400 CE to 1200 CE), [Aryabhata](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Aryabhata), [Brahmagupta](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Brahmagupta), [Bhaskara II](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bhaskara_II), [Varāhamihira](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Var%C4%81hamihira), and [Madhava](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Madhava_of_Sangamagrama) made important contributions. The [decimal number system](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Decimal) in use today[^cite_note-irfah346-514] was first recorded in Indian mathematics.[^cite_note-515] Indian mathematicians made early contributions to the study of the concept of [zero](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/0_\(number\)) as a number,[^cite_note-bourbaki46-516] [negative numbers](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Negative_numbers),[^cite_note-bourbaki49-517] [arithmetic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Arithmetic), and [algebra](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Algebra).[^cite_note-concise-britannica-518] [Trigonometry](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Trigonometry)[^cite_note-519] was further advanced in India, and the modern definitions of [sine](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sine) and [cosine](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cosine) were developed there.[^cite_note-520] These mathematical concepts were transmitted to the Middle East, China, and Europe.[^cite_note-concise-britannica-518] A later landmark in Indian mathematics was the development of the [series](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Series_\(mathematics\)) expansions for [trigonometric functions](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Trigonometric_function) (sine, cosine, and [arc tangent](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Arc_tangent)) by mathematicians of the [Kerala school](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kerala_school_of_astronomy_and_mathematics) in the 15th century CE. Their work, completed two centuries before the invention of [calculus](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Calculus) in Europe, provided the first example of a [power series](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Power_series).[^cite_note-521][^cite_note-522] In the modern era, [Srinivasa Ramanujan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Srinivasa_Ramanujan) made fundamental contributions to [number theory](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Number_theory).[^cite_note-hardy-ramanumajan-notice-523]&#x20;

### Music

Main article: [Music of India](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Music_of_India)

India contains a wide array of musical practices, including many different [folk musics](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Indian_folk_music) from different regions. [Indian classical music](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Indian_classical_music) has Vedic origins, and split in the 13th century into the two main traditions of [Hindustani](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hindustani_classical_music) and [Carnatic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Carnatic_music) music. Hindustani is associated with North India and is more [improvisational](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Musical_improvisation), featuring instruments such as the [sitar](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sitar) and [tabla](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tabla), and Carnatic is South Indian and more focused on written [compositions](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Musical_composition) such as the *[kriti](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kriti_\(music\))*, while both styles contain common elements such as the [raga](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Raga) melodic framework and [tala](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tala_\(music\)) rhythmic meter.[^cite_note-524] Indian music has influenced western genres, including [rock](25423.md) and [jazz](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jazz) musicians during the [1960s counterculture](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Counterculture_of_the_1960s).[^cite_note-525]&#x20;

[Indian film music](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Indian_film_music) is music written for [Indian cinema](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cinema_of_India), generally composed by [music directors](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_Indian_film_music_directors) and sung by [playback singers](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Playback_singers). Modern [Indian pop](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Indian_pop) takes influences from classical, folk, and western [pop music](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pop_music).[^cite_note-526]&#x20;

### Dance

Main article: [Dance in India](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dance_in_India)

Dance in India draws heavily on [Indian classical dance](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Indian_classical_dance) traditions. Many of these in turn arose in temples or other religious contexts. However, their sponsorship and promotion have continued in secular, modern India.[^cite_note-vatsyayan-lord-527][^cite_note-528] India also has local and modern dance traditions.[^cite_note-vatsyayan-lord-527] Whether a dance is classical is determined by the [Sangeet Natak Academi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sangeet_Natak_Academi), the Indian government's organisation for performing arts.[^cite_note-529] Although more dances could perhaps meet the criteria for classical, the Akademi has chosen eight.[^cite_note-530]&#x20;

| Dance | Origin state | Region | Type or origin | Musical accompaniment |
| --- | --- | --- | --- | --- |
| [Bharatanatyam](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bharatanatyam) | [Tamil Nadu](29918.md) | [South India](678583.md) | Temple dance | [Cinna Melam](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Melam), [Carnatic music](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Carnatic_music) |
| [Kathak](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kathak) | [Uttar Pradesh](231623.md) | [North India](1552939.md) | [Historical dance](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Historical_dance) | [Hindustani music](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hindustani_music) |
| [Kathakali](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kathakali) | [Kerala](4349459.md) | South India | Dance-drama | [Madhalam](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Madhalam) drum ensembles; [Sopana](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sopana_Sangeetham) vocal music |
| [Kuchipudi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kuchipudi) | [Andhra Pradesh](2377.md) | South India | Dance-drama | Carnatic music ensemble |
| [Manipuri](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Manipuri_dance) | [Manipur](422642.md) | [Northeast India](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Northeast_India) | Temple/ritual dance | Ensemble comprising [Pung Cholom](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pung_Cholom), flutes, trumpets, [Tambura](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tanbur), [Pena](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pena_\(musical_instrument\)), and cymbals |
| [Mohiniattam](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mohiniattam) | Kerala | South India | Dance-drama | Carnatic ensemble |
| [Odissi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Odissi) | [Odisha](250724.md) | [East India](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/East_India) | Temple dance | Ensemble of Hindustani music instruments: [pakhavaj](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pakhavaj), [sitar](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sitar), flute, cymbals, [harmonium](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Harmonium) |
| [Sattriya](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sattriya) | [Assam](186162.md) | Northeast India | Dance-drama | [Borgeet](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Borgeet) accompanied by [khol](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Khol) drums and cymbals. |

The best-known classical dance is Bharatanatyam, which began as temple dances performed by [Tamil](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tamil_people) [devadasis](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Devadasi).[^cite_note-vatsyayan-lord-527] Identified with "prostitutes and courtesans", their dancing was formally banned in 1947.[^cite_note-532] Concurrently, the dance was rehabilitated as a "pure" art form, with [Rukmini Devi Arundale](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rukmini_Devi_Arundale) as a prominent figure. A devdasi who went on to attain national and international prominence was [Thanjavur Balasaraswati](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Balasaraswati).[^cite_note-vatsyayan-lord-527] Some sources consider the dance-dramas [Chhau](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Chhau_dance) of [Jharkhand](197225.md), [West Bengal](34040.md), and [Odisha](250724.md) and [Yakshagana](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Yakshagana) of [Karnataka](16880.md) to also belong to the classical tradition.[^cite_note-burch-2013-533]&#x20;

Local dance traditions vary widely across India. In addition to the dance-dramas Chhau and Yakshagana, they include dance-dramas [Raslila](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Raslila) of western [Uttar Pradesh](231623.md) and [Terukkuttu](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Terukkuttu) of Tamil Nadu; calendrical and festival dances such as the [Bhangra](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bhangra_\(dance\)) of Punjab, especially at [Vaisakhi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Vaisakhi), the onset of spring, and [Garba](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Garba_\(dance\)) of [Gujarat](53707.md) during [Navratri](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Navratri); and tribal or [Adivasi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Adivasi) dances, such as those of the [Santal](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Santal) and [Toda people](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Toda_people), the latter, for example, in honour of the god [Ön](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Toda_people#Religion) who brought buffalo to earth.[^cite_note-vatsyayan-lord-527]&#x20;

Among 20th-century directions is the [modern dance](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Modern_dance) of [Uday Shankar](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Uday_Shankar) in which classical styles were employed but not adhered to rigidly. Examples are dance-dramas based on the ancient Indian [animal fables](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Animal_fable), *[Panchatantra](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Panchatantra)*, and Nehru's mid-century meditation on Indian history, *[The Discovery of India](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/The_Discovery_of_India)*.[^cite_note-vatsyayan-lord-527] Dance has been an essential aspect of Indian films from the first [talkies](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Talkies) of the 1930s. Individual and group dances in [Bollywood](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bollywood), for example, show a broad range of influences, including classical, local, and Western popular dance.[^cite_note-vatsyayan-lord-527] Towards the end of the 20th century, innovations in [British South Asian](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/British_South_Asian) music and dance, such as [Post-Bhangra](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Music_of_the_United_Kingdom_\(1990s\)#Post-Bhangra), fed back into dance in India.[^cite_note-vatsyayan-lord-527]&#x20;

### Clothing

Main article: [Clothing in India](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Clothing_in_India)



Women in [sari](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sari) at an adult literacy class in [Tamil Nadu](29918.md)

From ancient times until the advent of the modern era, the most widely worn traditional dress in India was [draped](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Draped_garment).[^cite_note-Tarlo1996-26-534] For women, it took the form of a [sari](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sari), a single piece of cloth many yards long.[^cite_note-Tarlo1996-26-534] The sari was traditionally wrapped around the lower body and the shoulder.[^cite_note-Tarlo1996-26-534] In its modern form, it is combined with an underskirt, or Indian [petticoat](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Petticoat#Asian_petticoats), and tucked in along the waistband for more secure fastening. It is also commonly worn with an Indian [blouse](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Blouse), or [choli](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Choli), which serves as the primary upper-body garment; the sari's end passes over the shoulder, covering the midriff and obscuring the upper body's contours.[^cite_note-Tarlo1996-26-534] For men, a similar but shorter length of cloth, the [dhoti](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dhoti), has served as a lower-body garment.[^cite_note-Tarlo1996-26-28-535]&#x20;

The use of stitched clothes became widespread after Muslim rule was established by the [Delhi Sultanate](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Delhi_Sultanate) (c. 1300 CE) and continued by the [Mughal Empire](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mughal_Empire) (c. 1525 CE).[^cite_note-Rahman-Alkazi2002-536] Among the garments introduced during this time and still commonly worn are: the [shalwars](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Shalwar_kameez) and [pyjamas](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pyjama), both styles of trousers, and the tunics [kurta](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kurta) and [kameez](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kameez).[^cite_note-Rahman-Alkazi2002-536] Shalwars are atypically wide at the waist but narrow to a cuffed bottom. A drawstring holds them up, causing them to pleat at the waist.[^cite_note-StevensonWaite2011-537] When the pants are cut quite narrow, on the [bias](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Grain_\(textile\)#Bias), they are called [churidars](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Churidar). The kameez is a long shirt or tunic.[^cite_note-StevensonWaite2011-b-538] Its side seams left open below the waistline.[^cite_note-539] The kurta is traditionally collarless and made of cotton or silk; it is worn plain or with embroidered decoration, such as [chikankari](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Chikankari); and typically falls to either just above or just below the wearer's knees.[^cite_note-Shukla2015-540]&#x20;

In the last 50 years, fashion in India has changed a great deal. In urban northern India, the sari is increasingly no longer everyday wear, though it remains popular on formal occasions. Younger urban women rarely wear the traditional shalwar kameez, favouring churidars or jeans. In office settings, ubiquitous air conditioning allows men to wear sports jackets year-round. For weddings and formal occasions, men in the middle and upper classes often wear bandhgala, or short [Nehru jackets](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nehru_jackets), with pants, with the groom and his [groomsmen](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Groomsmen) sporting [sherwanis](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sherwani) and churidars.[^cite_note-Dwyer2014-541]&#x20;

### Cuisine

Main article: [Indian cuisine](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Indian_cuisine)

A tandoor chef in [Turkman Gate](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Turkman_Gate), [Old Delhi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Old_Delhi), makes *khameri roti*, a Muslim-influenced style of [leavened bread](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bread#Leavening).

[\[ay\]](#cite_note-544)

[^cite_note-Panjabi1995-545]

A typical Indian meal is built on a plain cereal, complemented by savoury dishes.[^cite_note-Davidson2014-p409-546] The cooked cereal could be steamed rice; [chapati](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Chapati), a thin unleavened bread;[^cite_note-Davidson2014-p161-547] [idli](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Idli), a steamed breakfast cake; or [dosa](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dosa_\(food\)), a griddled pancake.[^cite_note-tamang-yeast-idlidosa-548] The savoury dishes might include [lentils](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lentil), [pulses](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pulses), vegetables, meat, poultry and fish commonly spiced with [ginger](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ginger_root) and [garlic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Garlic), but also [coriander](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Coriander), [cumin](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cumin), [turmeric](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Turmeric), [cinnamon](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cinnamon), [cardamom](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cardamom) and others.[^cite_note-Davidson2014-p409-546] In some instances, the ingredients may be mixed during the cooking process.[^cite_note-jhala-princely-biryani-549] India has distinctive vegetarian cuisines, each a feature of the geographical and cultural histories of its communities.[^cite_note-Davidson2014-p410-550] About 20% to 39% of India's population consists of vegetarians.[^cite_note-551][^cite_note-552] Although meat is eaten widely, the proportional consumption of meat is low.[^cite_note-SahakianSaloma2016-50-553]&#x20;

The most significant import of cooking techniques into India during the last millennium occurred during the [Mughal Empire](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mughal_Empire), spreading into northern India from regions to its northwest,[^cite_note-CollinghamCollingham2007-554] along with dishes such as [pilaf](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pilaf).[^cite_note-FOOTNOTERoger2000-555][^cite_note-sengupta-74-556] Onions, garlic, almonds, and spices were added to the simple yogurt marinade of Persia.[^cite_note-CollinghamCollingham2007-554] Rice was partially cooked and layered alternately with sauteed meat, the pot sealed tightly, and slow cooked according to another Persian cooking technique, to produce [biryani](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Biryani),[^cite_note-CollinghamCollingham2007-554] a feature of festive dining in many parts of India.[^cite_note-nandy2004-557]&#x20;

The diversity of Indian food served worldwide has been partially concealed by the dominance of [Punjabi cuisine](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Punjabi_cuisine). The popularity of [tandoori chicken](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tandoori_chicken)—cooked in a [tandoor](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tandoor) oven, which had traditionally been used for baking bread in the rural Punjab and the Delhi region, especially among Muslims, but which is originally from [Central Asia](6742.md)—dates to the 1950s, and was caused in large part by an entrepreneurial response among people from the Punjab who had been displaced by the 1947 partition.[^cite_note-Davidson2014-p410-550]&#x20;

### Sports

Main article: [Sport in India](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sport_in_India)

Sport in modern India is a nexus of little-known indigenous traditions and commercial sporting events with worldwide reach.[^cite_note-Prakash-OUP-Sports2021-562] Traditional sports such as *[kabaddi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kabaddi)* and *[kho-kho](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kho-kho)* have experienced a resurgence, attracting large television audiences;[^cite_note-Mukhopadhyay-RoutledgeSport-2026a-563] on the other hand, *[kushti](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kushti)* wrestling in rural *[akharas](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Akhara)*—often regarded by its practitioners as not so much a sport as a physical and moral regimen—has continued to survive away from the exposure of broadcasting.[^cite_note-Alter-WrestlersBody-1992-564] [Cricket](25675557.md) is India's most watched sport and a multi-billion dollar industry.[^cite_note-Mukhopadhyay-RoutledgeSport-2026b-565] The widespread popularity of chess among youth in [southern India](678583.md) culminated in 18-year-old [Gukesh Dommaraju](59333658.md) of [Chennai](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Chennai) winning the undisputed World Chess Champion title in 2024.[^cite_note-BBC-GukeshChamp-2024-566]&#x20;

[British intervention](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Company_rule_in_India) reshaped India's athletic landscape.[^cite_note-Sen-NationAtPlay-2015-567] It introduced cricket, [football](10568.md) and [field hockey](10886.md) and institutionalised [polo](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Polo)—a sport whose history is a case of reverse migration from India to the West.[^cite_note-dutta-polo2023-568][^cite_note-Sen-PoloMigration-2015-569] Today, at the rural [grassroots](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Grassroots) level, government initiatives like "[Khelo India](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Khelo_India)" have democratised athletics for traditionally overlooked youth.[^cite_note-IMPRI-KheloIndia-2026-570] Rural soccer programs have employed teamwork to break down old [caste](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Caste) barriers.[^cite_note-Philip-SoccerCaste-2022-571] State initiatives such as the [Target Olympic Podium Scheme](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Target_Olympic_Podium_Scheme) have expanded resources for female athletes; meanwhile, the prominent international victories of figures like boxer [Mary Kom](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mary_Kom) and badminton player [P. V. Sindhu](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/P._V._Sindhu) have challenged traditional gender stereotypes in sports.[^cite_note-Savani-IJPREMS-2025-572]&#x20;

[India](407754.md) has won eight men's cricket [ICC tournament](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/International_cricket#International_competitions) titles: two [Cricket World Cups](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cricket_World_Cup), three [T20 World Cups](8258172.md), and three [Champions Trophy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Champions_Trophy) titles.[^cite_note-573] [India](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/India_men%27s_national_field_hockey_team) has won a record eight men's field hockey gold medals in the [summer Olympics](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Field_hockey_at_the_Summer_Olympics), the last in [1980](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Field_hockey_at_the_1980_Summer_Olympics_%E2%80%93_Men%27s_tournament).[^cite_note-574]&#x20;

[^cite_note-115]: &#x20;"All these achievements are dwarfed, though, by the Sanskrit linguistic tradition culminating in the famous grammar by Pāṇini, known as the Aṣṭhādhyāyī. The elegance and comprehensiveness of its architecture have yet to be surpassed by any grammar of any language, and its ingenious methods of stratifying out use and mention, language and metalanguage, and theorem and metatheorem predate key discoveries in western philosophy by millennia."<sup>[\[95\]](#cite_note-Evans-2009-114)</sup>

[^cite_note-117]: &#x20;[Classical Sanskrit](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Classical_Sanskrit), a refined and standardised grammatical form would emerge in the mid-1st millennium BCE and was codified in the *[Aṣṭādhyāyī](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/A%E1%B9%A3%E1%B9%AD%C4%81dhy%C4%81y%C4%AB)* ('Eight chapters') of [Pāṇini](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/P%C4%81%E1%B9%87ini).<sup>[\[t\]](#cite_note-115)</sup> The two major Sanskrit epics, the [Mahābhārata](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mah%C4%81bh%C4%81rata) and the [Rāmāyaṇa](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/R%C4%81m%C4%81ya%E1%B9%87a), however, were composed in a range of oral storytelling registers called [Epic Sanskrit](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Epic_Sanskrit) which was used in northern India between 400 BCE and 300 CE, and roughly contemporary with classical Sanskrit.<sup>[\[96\]](#cite_note-Lowe2017-epic-116)</sup>

[^cite_note-118]: &#x20;the further developed mid-first-millennium form of the historic religion of the [Vedas](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Vedas), and the precursor to [Hinduism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hinduism)

[^cite_note-120]: &#x20;Chronicling the life of the Buddha was central to the beginnings of recorded history in India.<sup>[\[97\]](#cite_note-FOOTNOTEStein201021,_61–62-119)</sup>

[^cite_note-123]: &#x20;The Mauryan economy was helped by the rise of Buddhism and Jainism, creeds that promoted nonviolence, proscribed ostentation, or superfluous sacrifices and rituals, and reduced the costs of economic transactions; by coinage that increased economic accommodation in the region; and by the use of writing, which might have boosted more intricate business dealings.<sup>[\[99\]](#cite_note-FOOTNOTERoy201228-122)</sup>

[^cite_note-125]: &#x20;To promote movement and trade, the Mauryans built roads, most prominently a chiefly winter-time road—the Uttarapath—which connected eastern Afghanistan to their capital [Pataliputra](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Patliputra), during the time of year when the water levels in the intersecting rivers were low, and they could be easily [forded](30432825.md).<sup>[\[100\]](#cite_note-FOOTNOTEIori2023184,_219-124)</sup>

[^cite_note-129]: &#x20;Because the edicts forbade both the killing of wild animals and the destruction of forests, some modern environmental historians see Ashoka as an early embodiment of that ethos.<sup>[\[102\]](#cite_note-elverskog-2020-lead-1-127)</sup><sup>[\[103\]](#cite_note-FOOTNOTEFisher201872-128)</sup>

[^cite_note-139]: &#x20;No ruler of this period was able to create an empire and consistently control lands much beyond their core region. When [Harsha](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Harsha) of [Kannauj](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kannauj), who ruled much of the Indo-Gangetic Plain from 606 to 647 CE, attempted to expand southwards, he was defeated by the [Chalukya](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Chalukya_dynasty) ruler of the Deccan. When his successor attempted to expand eastwards, he was defeated by the [Pala](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pala_Empire) king of [Bengal](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bengal). When the Chalukyas attempted to expand southwards, they were defeated by the [Pallavas](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pallava_dynasty) from farther south, who in turn were opposed by the [Pandyas](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pandya_dynasty) and the [Cholas](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Chola_dynasty) from still farther south.<sup>[\[112\]](#cite_note-FOOTNOTEStein2010115–116-138)</sup>

[^cite_note-143]: &#x20;Temple towns of various sizes began to appear everywhere as India underwent another urbanisation.<sup>[\[115\]](#cite_note-FOOTNOTEStein2010118–119-142)</sup>

[^cite_note-144]: &#x20;Lands that are today part of [Thailand](30128.md),[Cambodia](334751.md), [Vietnam](202354.md), and [Indonesia](14579.md)

[^cite_note-146]: &#x20;Indian merchants, scholars, and sometimes armies were involved in this transmission; Southeast Asians took the initiative as well, with many sojourning in Indian seminaries and translating Buddhist and Hindu texts into their languages.<sup>[\[116\]](#cite_note-FOOTNOTEStein2010121–123-145)</sup>

[^cite_note-223]: &#x20;The northernmost point under Indian control is the disputed [Siachen Glacier](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Siachen_Glacier) in [Jammu and Kashmir](61449812.md); however, the [Government of India](553883.md) regards the entire region of the former princely state of Jammu and Kashmir, including [Gilgit-Baltistan](18807336.md) administered by Pakistan, to be its territory. It therefore assigns the latitude 37° 6′ to its northernmost point.

[^cite_note-436]: &#x20;Caste is a form of [social stratification](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Social_stratification) characterised by [endogamy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Endogamy), hereditary transmission of an occupation, ritual status in a hierarchy, and customary social interaction or exclusion defined by cultural notions of purity and pollution.<sup>[\[397\]](#cite_note-caste-lead-435)</sup>

[^cite_note-460]: &#x20;The vast majority of India's 1.4 billion population are Hindus; 13.4 per cent are Muslims, 80 per cent of whom are Sunni Muslims; 2.5 per cent are Christian; and less than 0.0004 per cent are Jews. The majority of the 3,000 Jews remaining in India today live in Maharashtra, namely in Mumbai, the Konkan villages and Pune. There are small remnants of Jewish communities in Ahmedabad, New Delhi and Calcutta; only five Jews remain in 'Jew Town' in Mattancherry, Cochin, and a dozen or so more are scattered around Kerala. Even in their heyday before 1950, the Indian Jews from all three Jewish groups – Cochin Jews, Bene Israel and Baghdadis – only numbered 28,000 souls. They neither suffered from anti-Semitism at the hand of their fellow countrymen – Hindu, Christian, Parsee or other – nor experienced ethnic tensions with Indian Moslems. Somehow, they created and maintained unique Indo-Judaic customs, which they enacted in the multi-religious and complex society of India. The Baghdadi Jews never numbered more than 8,000 souls; today, just 70 remain in all of India."<sup>[\[349\]](#cite_note-weil-2019-387)</sup>

[^cite_note-464]: &#x20;[Gavin Flood](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gavin_Flood) has defined Hinduism to be "a range and history of practice" which references the texts and sacrifices in the [Vedas](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Vedas); whose adherents belong to [endogamous](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Endogamy) social units ([jāti](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/J%C4%81ti)/[varna](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Varna_\(Hinduism\))); participate in rituals in which offerings are made to a deity and blessings received in return ([puja](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Puja_\(Hinduism\))); and profess an outwardly polytheism, but sometimes also an inwardly monotheism in which many gods are considered manifestations of one supreme being.<sup>[\[422\]](#cite_note-flood-hindu-practice-462)</sup> [Axel Michaels](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Axel_Michaels) has echoed the assessment, "I propose that Hindu India is special in that rituals surpass belief ...."<sup>[\[422\]](#cite_note-flood-hindu-practice-462)</sup> Islamic religious life in India, according to [Barbara D. Metcalf](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Barbara_D._Metcalf), has a central [Sufi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sufism) tradition of inner spirituality, "cultivation of ethical norms," and a reverence for holy guides. There is also text-based scholarship, especially the study of prophetic traditions in the [hadith](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hadith).<sup>[\[423\]](#cite_note-Metcalf2009-Islam-463)</sup>

[^cite_note-469]: &#x20;The tenets of Hindu and Muslim mystics were near enough that some Hindus were receptive to the sermons of Sufi masters (*[pir](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pir_\(Sufism\))*&#x73;), and syncretic movements arose.<sup>[\[427\]](#cite_note-heterodoxy-indian-sufism-468)</sup>

[^cite_note-mod-human-migration-35]: &#x20; [Petraglia & Allchin 2007](#CITEREFPetragliaAllchin2007), p. [10](https://books.google.com/books?id=Qm9GfjNlnRwC\&pg=PA10): "Y-Chromosome and Mt-DNA data support the colonization of South Asia by modern humans originating in Africa. \[...] Coalescence dates for most non-European populations average to between 73 and 55 ka." [Dyson 2018](#CITEREFDyson2018), p. [1](https://books.google.com/books?id=3TRtDwAAQBAJ\&pg=PA1): "Modern human beings—*Homo sapiens*—originated in Africa. Then, intermittently, sometime between 60,000 and 80,000 years ago, tiny groups of them began to enter the north-west of the Indian subcontinent. It seems likely that initially they came by way of the coast. \[...] it is virtually certain that there were *Homo sapiens* in the subcontinent 55,000 years ago, even though the earliest fossils that have been found of them date to only about 30,000 years before the present." [Fisher 2018](#CITEREFFisher2018), p. [23](https://books.google.com/books?id=kZVuDwAAQBAJ\&pg=PA23): "Scholars estimate that the first successful expansion of the *Homo sapiens* range beyond Africa and across the Arabian Peninsula occurred from as early as 80,000 years ago to as late as 40,000 years ago, although there may have been prior unsuccessful emigrations. Some of their descendants extended the human range ever further in each generation, spreading into each habitable land they encountered. One human channel was along the warm and productive coastal lands of the Persian Gulf and northern Indian Ocean. Eventually, various bands entered India between 75,000 years ago and 35,000 years ago." [Mattern 2019](#CITEREFMattern2019), pp. 146–147 All non-African human populations share a common ancestry in the recent past, about 80,000 to 50,000 years ago. Some time within this period, a group of modern humans migrated from Africa, traversed southern Asia, and crossed the sea to the ancient continent of Sahul (now Australia, Tasmania, and Papua New Guinea), picking up Neanderthal and Denisovan genes along the way; branches reached Europe somewhat later, and Americas last. This was not the first migration of anatomically modern humans out of Africa. ... But for the most part, earlier populations of humans around Eurasia sputtered out, returned to Africa, or were replaced by humans from the last migration out of Africa.

[^cite_note-Combined-2-36]: &#x20; [Dyson 2018](#CITEREFDyson2018), pp. [4–5](https://books.google.com/books?id=3TRtDwAAQBAJ\&pg=PA4). [Fisher 2018](#CITEREFFisher2018), p. [33](https://books.google.com/books?id=kZVuDwAAQBAJ\&pg=PA23).

[^cite_note-archaic-sansk-37]: &#x20; [Jamison & Brereton 2014](#CITEREFJamisonBrereton2014), p. 5 "For the northwest, which comprises the geographic horizon of the R̥gveda, iron began to be manufactured around 1200–1000 bce. The R̥gvedic hymns, therefore, would have to have been composed no later than this period... The R̥gveda is the surface of a very long tradition." [Lowe 2015](#CITEREFLowe2015), pp. 1–2 "[It](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rigveda) consists of 1,028 hymns (*sūktas*), highly crafted poetic compositions originally intended for recital during rituals and for the invocation of and communication with the Indo-Aryan gods. Modern scholarly opinion largely agrees that these hymns were composed between around 1500 BCE and 1200 BCE, during the eastward migration of the Indo-Aryan tribes from the mountains of what is today northern Afghanistan across the Punjab into north India." [Witzel 2003](#CITEREFWitzel2003), pp. 68–70: "The RV text was composed before the introduction and massive use of iron, that is before ca. 1200–1000 BCE."

[^cite_note-hinduism-elements-38]: &#x20;\* [Jamison & Brereton 2020](#CITEREFJamisonBrereton2020), pp. 2, 4–5: "The Ṛgveda is one of the four Vedas, which together constitute the oldest texts in Sanskrit and the earliest evidence for what will become Hinduism. \[...] Although Vedic religion is very different in many regards from what is known as Classical Hinduism, the seeds are there." [Flood 2020](#CITEREFFlood2020), p. 4, see note 4: "I take the term 'Hinduism' to meaningfully denote a range and history of practice characterised by a number of features, particularly reference to Vedic textual and sacrificial origins, ..." [Michaels 2017](#CITEREFMichaels2017), p. 86: "Almost all traditional Hindu families observe until today at least three *samskaras* (initiation, marriage, and death ritual). ...Although *samskaras* vary from region to region, from class (*varna*) to class, and from caste to caste, their core elements remain the same owing to the common source, the Veda, and a common priestly tradition preserved by the *Brahmin* priests." [Fisher 2018](#CITEREFFisher2018), pp. 59–60 " Major parts of what is known today as Hinduism are largely based on this Vedic foundation; some Vedic hymns are still chanted as vital components of Brahminic Hindu rituals, including weddings and funerals. Hence, the Vedas comprise the longest continuously used body of sacred texts in human history and are an unmatched source for this early period’s environmental history." [Truschke 2025](#CITEREFTruschke2025), p. 18 "Vedic rituals, social organization, relationships with certain animals, and languages developed and spread over later centuries, ultimately numbering among the many sources for what we now call Hinduism."

[^cite_note-Combined-IA-Dravidian-39]: &#x20; [Dyson 2018](#CITEREFDyson2018), p. 25. "Dravidian languages were once spoken throughout much of the subcontinent. ... it is notable that the boundary which separates Indo-Aryan from Dravidian-speaking areas still runs on a slightly south-west to north-east gradient. ... But the origin of this process of Indo-Aryan expansion may well lie before the first millennium BCE." [Truschke 2025](#CITEREFTruschke2025), p. 22 "The Rig Veda outlines two distinct communities that intermixed: the migrants who revered Vedic hymns and rituals and those they name as *dasa* or *dasyu*, autochthonous residents of northern India. ... Language provides the most visceral evidence of cross-cultural exchanges. Many, possibly all, *dasa* groups spoke Dravidian and Munda languages, two linguistic families that preceded Indo-European tongues on the subcontinent."

[^cite_note-late-vedic-caste-40]: &#x20; [Dyson 2018](#CITEREFDyson2018), pp. 16–17 "... the *Arya* used fire to help with forest clearance, and the later introduction of iron axes must also have helped. ... a widespread process of urbanization ... began to occur in the Ganges basin between about 600 BCE and 400 BCE. Thus, by the latter date, there were a number of significant—mostly riverside—cities scattered throughout the basin.... they became the centres for social, economic, political, and religious developments. They were also places of evident social differentiation. Thus, by 400 BCE, the essential structural features of the caste system already existed." [Kulke & Rothermund 2016](#CITEREFKulkeRothermund2016), p. 64 "... the indigenous people subjected by the Aryans possessed great skills as artisans. Racial discrimination against these dark-skinned people also led to a discrimination against the trades which they plied. The original lack of such skills among the Vedic Aryans was probably one of the most important reasons for the emergence of the caste system, which was designed to maintain the social and political superiority of the Aryans. ... The late Vedic social stratification foreshadowed the rigid varna and gender hierarchy of later centuries when caste and the subordination of women became two sides of the same coin." [Truschke 2025](#CITEREFTruschke2025), p. 76 "Three interrelated developments—dated between about 300 BCE and 150 CE—help to explain how Vyasa’s *[Mahabharata](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mahabharata)* burst onto the scene in northern India sometime around 100 or 200 CE: the geographical and political expansion of Sanskrit, a more robust hereditary caste system, and Brahminical integration of non-Vedic ideas."

[^cite_note-Fisher2018-67–68-41]: &#x20; [Fisher 2018](#CITEREFFisher2018), pp. 67–68. "These two religious movements advocated similar social structures for humans, with individual achievement of total nonviolence and compassion toward all living creatures as the basis of status. ... Consequently, any individual who practices nonviolence and [dana](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/D%C4%81na) could rise socially and morally."

[^cite_note-maurya-gupta-42]: &#x20; [Dyson 2018](#CITEREFDyson2018), pp. 16–17 "Pataliputra became the centre of the loose-knit Mauryan 'Empire'". [Ludden 2014](#CITEREFLudden2014), pp. 29–30. "The geography of the Mauryan Empire resembled a spider with a small dense body and long spindly legs." [Fisher 2018](#CITEREFFisher2018), p. 67 "Even in the Gangetic plain, ... most of this vast territory was ruled by largely autonomous kings who nominally recognized or just paid tribute to the Gupta emperor." [Robb 2011](#CITEREFRobb2011), pp. 56–57 "Nevertheless the Gupta empire remained more of a confederacy than a centralized state."

[^cite_note-creative-explosion-43]: &#x20; [Ludden 2014](#CITEREFLudden2014), pp. 28–29 "A creative explosion in all the arts was a most remarkable feature of this ancient transformation, a permanent cultural legacy. Mauryan territory was created in its day by awesome armies and dreadful war, but future generations would cherish its beautiful pillars, inscriptions, coins, sculptures, buildings, ceremonies, and texts, particularly later Buddhist writers." [Van Brummelen 2014](#CITEREFVan_Brummelen2014), pp. 46–48 "The story of the growth of arithmetic from the ancient inheritance to the wealth passed on to the Renaissance is dramatic and passes through several cultures. The most groundbreaking achievement was the evolution of a positional number system, ... Roughly speaking, this began in India, was transmitted to Islam, and then to the Latin West."

[^cite_note-declining-status-44]: &#x20; [Dyson 2018](#CITEREFDyson2018), p. 20 "Therefore, by the time of the Mauryan Empire the position of women in mainstream Indo-Aryan society seems to have deteriorated." [Stein 2010](#CITEREFStein2010), p. 90 "Darkness can be said to have pervaded one aspect of society during the inter-imperial centuries: the degradation of women." [Ramusack 1999](#CITEREFRamusack1999), pp. 27–29 "The legal rights, as well as the ideal images, of women were increasingly circumscribed during the Gupta era." [Truschke 2025](#CITEREFTruschke2025), p. 115 "During the early to mid-first millennium CE, life got worse for many Indians—especially women and lower classes—as inequality became further systematized and entrenched."

[^cite_note-gupta-untouchability-45]: &#x20; [Kulke & Rothermund 2016](#CITEREFKulkeRothermund2016), pp. 125–126 "[Faxian](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Faxian) ... also recorded evidence of the caste system as he could observe it. According to this evidence the treatment meted out to untouchables such as the Chandalas was very similar to that which they experienced in later periods. This would contradict assertions that this rigid form of the caste system emerged in India only as a reaction to the Islamic conquest." [Truschke 2025](#CITEREFTruschke2025), p. 124 "Still, Brahmins and other upper castes often enforced purity boundaries, which gave birth to a harsh practice first attested in the mid-first millennium CE and that persists in some South Asian communities today: untouchability. ... had two major forms: temporary (e.g., menstruating women) and permanent (e.g., those born lower caste or outside the caste system, ...). Both kinds were oppressive, and the latter resulted in segregated living."

[^cite_note-Influence_Southeast_Asia-46]: &#x20; [Asher & Talbot 2022](#CITEREFAsherTalbot2022), p. 20"The victory of the Chola fleet ... was the apex of Indian influence in Southeast Asia, which had assimilated many elements of Indian civilization over the past six or more centuries, including the Sanskrit language, South Indian scripts, and the religions of Hinduism and Buddhism." [Dyson 2018](#CITEREFDyson2018), p. 40 "On the east coast, the kingdoms of the Pallavas and Cholas were important in ... the 'Indianization' of south-east Asia. ... the emergence of 'Indian kingdoms' in what are now Cambodia, Indonesia, Thailand, and Vietnam." [Fisher 2018](#CITEREFFisher2018), p. 83 " South Indian kings, trade, and culture continued to extend overseas as far east as Java (today in Indonesia) where Buddhist and Hindu architecture and cultural features remain." [Truschke 2025](#CITEREFTruschke2025), p. 176 "Alongside trade, aspects of Indian culture and religions continued to circulate in Southeast Asia. Hindu artefacts have been found in southern Vietnam, including hundreds of small golden plaques and deities made throughout much of the first millennium CE. More spectacularly, the twelfth-century Cambodian ruler [Suryavarman II](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Suryavarman_II), who maintained diplomatic relations with the Cholas, built the large temple complex of [Angkor Wat](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Angkor_Wat) that still draws many visitors today. The oldest [Javanese Ramayana](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kakawin_Ramayana) was penned in the tenth century based largely on a ca. 600 retelling of Rama’s tale in Sanskrit.”

[^cite_note-first-millennium_coastal_religions-47]: &#x20; [Ludden 2014](#CITEREFLudden2014), p. 52 "All along the Indian Ocean coast, Zoroastrians, Christians, Arabs, and Jews became well established." [Asher & Talbot 2022](#CITEREFAsherTalbot2022), pp. 91–92 "... maritime trade along the Malabar coast had always been in the hands of immigrant trading communities. Two ... were the Syrian Christians and the Jews, who had been resident in Kerala probably hundreds of years before ... the ninth and late tenth centuries, respectively." [Fisher 2018](#CITEREFFisher2018), pp. 88–89 "Jewish, Christian, and Muslim communities within ports often prospered through Indian Ocean trade diaspora networks." [Truschke 2025](#CITEREFTruschke2025), pp. 155, 178 "(p. 155) By around 700 CE, followers of two additional religions, namely Parsis and Muslims, had entered the subcontinent. Parsis follow the ancient religion of Zoroastrianism, which is about as old as Vedic traditions (p. 178) By the ninth century, the Malabar coast hosted Arab Muslim, Jewish, Persian, and Indian communities."

[^cite_note-swift-horse-48]: &#x20; [Ludden 2014](#CITEREFLudden2014), p. 86 "Central Asian warriors became supreme during South Asia's medieval transition by deploying swift-horse cavalry skilled in firing arrows at full gallop, volley after volley; ..." [Asher & Talbot 2022](#CITEREFAsherTalbot2022), pp. 22, 31–32."(p.22) Due to their nomadic background, the Turkic peoples were skilled at cavalry warfare. (p.31–32) Employing a classic nomadic tactic of the Central Asian steppes, their light cavalry could fan out and flank the enemy from all sides,..." [Truschke 2025](#CITEREFTruschke2025), p. 182 "The Ghurids benefitted from upswings in military technology in the eleventh–twelfth centuries. Specifically, wider use of nailed shoes and stirrups made horses nimbler in warfare."

[^cite_note-Islamic-cosmopolis-49]: &#x20; [Asher & Talbot 2022](#CITEREFAsherTalbot2022), p. 60 "In the long term, however, the cultural and social enrichment that resulted from participation in the world's most cosmopolitan civilization of the middle ages was to become an inextricable part of India's greatness" [Truschke 2025](#CITEREFTruschke2025), p. 164 "Whatever vocabulary we use to describe Muslim and, later, Indo-Muslim political figures, these groups brought new languages, cultural mores, and historical sources—in addition to a new religion—to the already diverse Indian subcontinent.”

[^cite_note-vijayanagara-character-legacy-50]: &#x20; [Asher & Talbot 2022](#CITEREFAsherTalbot2022), pp. 62, 75, 85. "(p. 62) Vijayanagara ... is often credited with preserving a distinctly Hindu way of life in south India that had been lost in the north, a misconception that overlooks both the creativity and cosmopolitan nature of the Vijayanagara elite. (p. 75) The Vijayanagara kings did not see themselves as engaged in mortal combat for the survival of Hinduism and South Indian society. However, the rulers of Vijayanagara did attempt to act as righteous kings behaving according to dharma, that is, who lived up to traditional Indic expectations of rulers. ... (p. 85) Much of the growing cosmopolitanism of South India occurred because people increasingly moved around within the large area where Vijayanagara power held sway." [Fisher 2018](#CITEREFFisher2018), pp. 97–98 "In the dry uplands of the southwestern Deccan, the overarching military-fiscal conquest state of Vijayanagar (“City of Victory”) prevailed for centuries. ... Vijayanagar’s capital (on the Tungabhadra River) featured royal palaces and temples which brought prestige and culturally adhered people to its regime through displays of ritual submission by surrounding rulers and merchants." [Metcalf & Metcalf 2012](#CITEREFMetcalfMetcalf2012), p. 47 "Vijayanagar, based in Karnataka but soon expanding into Andhra and beyond, was founded by brothers who ... espoused a strong Shaivite tradition, that of worshipping Lord Shiva, as an ideology ... That kingdom emulated the military technology of the Sultanate, ..."

[^cite_note-Sikhism-emergence-51]: &#x20; [Asher & Talbot 2022](#CITEREFAsherTalbot2022), p. 343 " ... founder of Sikhism, Guru Nanak (1469–1539). Like Kabir, Guru Nanak was part of the Sant tradition ... that rejected institutionalized forms of religion." [Metcalf & Metcalf 2012](#CITEREFMetcalfMetcalf2012), p. 13 "Like the sufis, the bhakti teachers emphasized the individual's own devotion to the divine. … Kabir and Nanak, emphasized worship of a personal God without form. In so doing they distanced themselves from distinctive Hindu and Muslim symbols." [Stein 2010](#CITEREFStein2010), p. 170 “Sikhism … was founded by Guru Nanak in the religious revival of the fifteenth and sixteenth centuries. He preached a doctrine of universal toleration and seeking what was good and common in all religion, and recruited followers among Hindus and Muslims….” [Kulke & Rothermund 2016](#CITEREFKulkeRothermund2016), pp. 232–233 "Around the fourteenth century a number of Sants or saint-poets emerged in north India. They questioned the usefulness of the Brahmans/priests, rituals and the caste system. … Guru Nanak, the founder of Sikhism, also emerged from a similar socio-religious background and his ideas were enriched by both Hindu and Islamic currents of thought." [Gilbert 2017](#CITEREFGilbert2017), p. 79 "... a religious leader in the northwest of the subcontinent, Guru Nanak (1469–1539), received a revelation from God urging him to bring Hindus and Muslims together. His teachings and those of his immediate successors led to the emergence of an egalitarian faith called Sikhism."

[^cite_note-AsherAsher2022-187-52]: &#x20;[Asher & Talbot 2022](#CITEREFAsherTalbot2022), p. 187 "Above all, the long period of relative peace ushered in by Akbar's power, and maintained by his successors throughout the seventeenth century, contributed to India's economic expansion."

[^cite_note-metcalf-mughal-centralised-53]: &#x20;[Metcalf & Metcalf 2012](#CITEREFMetcalfMetcalf2012), pp. 54–55 "A further key to Akbar's successes were the administrative reforms that created an enduring framework for rule. ...Nobles were awarded ranks, known as mansab, demarcated decimally, and were expected to provide horsemen according to the rank number for the emperor's use. They were appointed to positions in two parallel hierarchies, one with civil responsibilities and one military – thus a check on each other – at levels from district, to province, to centre, throughout the empire. ... Even a relatively centralized empire like this, however, was limited in its reach into local communities. Mughal officials typically negotiated for delivery of the revenue demand with lineage heads and chieftains, homogenized in Mughal usage as [zamindars](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Zamindar) (land-holders)."

[^cite_note-mughal-cultural-synthesis-54]: &#x20; [Asher & Talbot 2022](#CITEREFAsherTalbot2022), pp. 178 "Chronicles, both visual and written, tell us that instrumentalists from Khurasan and Central Asia as well as singers from India resided in Akbar's court. ... By far the most famous singer was the legendary [Tansen](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tansen) (d. 1586), whom Akbar acquired from a subject prince early in his reign in 1562." [Truschke 2025](#CITEREFTruschke2025), p. 255 "Mughal translations provided many Hindus with access to their religious texts between the sixteenth and nineteenth centuries. Few early modern people knew Sanskrit, a language that was never a common vernacular and, even as a literary tongue, had long been limited to an educated elite. In contrast, many Indians of diverse religious backgrounds learned Persian to work for the Mughal state." [Naim 1978](#CITEREFNaim1978) "The poems of [Ghālib](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mirza_Ghalib) and [Mīr](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mir_Taqi_Mir) represent the best of the [Urdu](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Urdu) [ghazal](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ghazal); and the Urdu ghazal, as an anonymous wit has remarked, is the Muslims' greatest gift to India, after the Tāj Mahal." [Fisher 2018](#CITEREFFisher2018), p. 106 " For nearly four centuries, the Taj Mahal has stood as an architectural masterpiece, famous worldwide for the technology of its construction, and, even more, for the quality of its workmanship and the exquisite balance and proportion of its forms." [Asher 1992](#CITEREFAsher1992), pp. 250–251 "More than any other ruler, Shah Jahan sought to use architecture to project the emperor's formal and semi-divine character. ... Nowhere is this seen more clearly than in his *de novo* city, [Shahjahanabad](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Shahjahanabad), ... The number of surviving structures is great, reminding us that the mechanisms of Mughal economy encouraged constant spending and cash flow. ... the elegant imperial taste ... is widely emulated across India."

[^cite_note-roy-princely-60]: &#x20;[Roy 2020](#CITEREFRoy2020), p. 242–243 "... most (princely) states in the late nineteenth century understood by economic prosperity a concept very similar to how British India saw economic development—the growth of commerce and cultivation. Railways and irrigation were the key instruments to achieve these aims. But promoting trade, building railways, and supplying irrigation water to cultivators were all challenges more difficult to meet in the states. This was so because most of them were small in size, situated in hilly land, dry and landlocked, and without perennial rivers. When possible they built railways and joined them with the network of lines that connected the port cities of British India. The nineteenth-century globalization, together with initial differences in geographical endowments, favored British India more than the princely states."

[^cite_note-Metcalf-States-72]: &#x20;[Metcalf & Metcalf 2012](#CITEREFMetcalfMetcalf2012), pp. 275–276 " Confronted with Nehru's obstinate opposition, the Andhra leader Potti Sriramalu began a fast to the death in October 1952. In December Sriramalu died; three days later the government agreed to establish an Andhra state. Once begun, the process of reorganization could not be stopped. A States Reorganization Commission was set up, and its report, implemented in 1956, brought into existence a comprehensively reordered India, divided into fourteen states on the basis of language. ... States reorganization encouraged a new regional linguistic politics, yet at the same time, by peaceably accommodating an intensely felt popular sentiment, the move helped stem separatist enthusiasm."

The actual term Hindu first occurs as a Persian geographical term for the people who lived beyond the Indus River. The term Hindu originated as a geographical term and did not refer to a religion. Later, Hindu was taken by European languages from the Arabic term al-Hind, which referred to the people who lived across the Indus River. This Arabic term was itself taken from the Persian term Hindū, which refers to all Indians. By the 13th century, Hindustan emerged as a popular alternative name for India, meaning the "land of Hindus."

[^cite_note-delhi-sultanate-147]: &#x20; [Ludden 2014](#CITEREFLudden2014), p. 85 "Muhammad Ghuri died in 1206. His trusted ... general, Qutb ud din Aibak, governor of Delhi, declared independence. His dynasty was the first in a series that became known as the Delhi Sultanate. ... The Delhi Sultanate became an epoch-making Indian dynasty by repelling the Mongols, who were unstoppable elsewhere in Asia."

[^cite_note-zimmi-148]: &#x20; [Metcalf & Metcalf 2012](#CITEREFMetcalfMetcalf2012), p. 41 "The subjects of the dynasties were primarily non-Muslim, designated as *[zimmi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dhimmi)*, 'protected people', left to their own law and customs. They were, in principle, liable to a [capitation tax](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Poll_tax) (*[jizya](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jizya)*) but not subject to military conscription. Law, generally, was administered according to the law of the parties or, if they differed, that of the defendant." ([Asher & Talbot 2022](#CITEREFAsherTalbot2022), p. 54 "The bulk of the non-Muslim population of India was largely left to pursue its religions in peace because had been classified as zimmis or protected subjects by the first Islamic rulers in the subcontinent, the eighth-century Arabs of Sind, and this practice was followed by subsequent Muslim kings."

[^cite_note-mongols-repulsed-149]: &#x20; [Ludden 2014](#CITEREFLudden2014), p. 85 "The Delhi Sultanate became an epoch-making Indian dynasty by repelling the Mongols, who were unstoppable elsewhere in Asia."

[^cite_note-indo-islamic-150]: &#x20; [Asher & Talbot 2022](#CITEREFAsherTalbot2022), pp. 58–59 "The most important result of the establishment of the Delhi Sultanate was the incorporation of South Asia into the growing geographic sphere of Islam. Ties between north India and the eastern Persian world were reinforced ... Through its integration into this expansive realm, within which a common religion and culture circulated widely, India itself became a considerably more cosmopolitan place."

[^cite_note-sultanate-vijaya-151]: &#x20; [Asher & Talbot 2022](#CITEREFAsherTalbot2022), p. 62 "Another significant result of Delhi’s military expeditions was the destruction of the existing regional kingdoms of the south. This paved the way for the emergence of Vijayanagara, a new state ruled by indigenous warriors that shaped the society and culture of south India for centuries thereafter."

[^cite_note-FOOTNOTERobb201197–98-152]: &#x20;[Robb 2011](#CITEREFRobb2011), pp. 97–98.

[^cite_note-asher-talbot-akbar-162]: &#x20;[Asher & Talbot 2022](#CITEREFAsherTalbot2022), p. 187 "Akbar's economic policies stimulated the growth of commercial activity, which interconnected the various parts of South Asia in increasingly close networks. His stipulation that land taxes be paid in cash forced peasants into the market networks where they could obtain the necessary money, while the standardization of the imperial currency made the exchange of goods for money easier. Above all, the long period of relative peace ushered in by Akbar's power, and maintained by his successors throughout the seventeenth century, contributed to India's economic expansion."

[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEAsherTalbot2022231-163]: &#x20;[Asher & Talbot 2022](#CITEREFAsherTalbot2022), p. 231.
